\mathcal{D}H\rightarrow\mathcal{D}H
, the functional integral in equation (
80
) becomes

\displaystyle\int\Big\{\prod_{\mu}\delta[C_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}(H)]\det\!\Big(-\frac{1}{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}\partial^{2}\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\times e^{iS[\bar{\psi},\psi,A,H]/(\hbar c)}\mathcal{D}\phi\mathcal{D}\bar{\psi}\mathcal{D}\psi\mathcal{D}A\mathcal{D}H.

(93)

Here we have defined the functional measure of the gravity gauge functions as
\mathcal{D}\phi=\prod_{\mu}\mathcal{D}\phi_{\mu}
.

Next, we express the functional delta functions and the Faddeev–Popov determinants in equation (
93
) as Lagrangian densities. The functional delta functions in equation (
93
) are rewritten as

\displaystyle\prod_{\mu}\delta[C_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}(H)]

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{g}}\prod_{\mu}\int\exp\Big(\frac{i}{4\kappa\hbar c\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}\int w^{(\mu)}w_{(\mu)}d^{4}x\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\delta[C_{\mathrm{g}}^{(\mu)}(H)-w^{(\mu)}]\mathcal{D}w^{(\mu)}

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{g}}\exp\!\Big(\frac{i}{4\kappa\hbar c\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}\int\!C_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}(H)C_{\mathrm{g}\mu}(H)d^{4}x\Big)

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{g}}\exp\!\Big(\frac{i}{\hbar c}\!\int\!\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}d^{4}x\Big).

(94)

Here
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}
is the gravity gauge-fixing parameter,
N_{\mathrm{g}}
is an unimportant normalization constant, and
w^{\mu}
are arbitrary scalar functions. In analogy with the QED gauge fixing in section
7.2.1
, the gravity gauge-fixing parameter
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}
is any finite real-valued constant. The choice of
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1
corresponds to the harmonic gauge condition in equation (
90
). For a discussion of this correspondence, see section 4.2 of the supplementary material. In the second equality of equation (
94
), we have used the functional delta function to integrate over
w^{\mu}
. The last equality of equation (
94
) defines the gauge-fixing Lagrangian density of unified gravity, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}C_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}(H)C_{\mathrm{g}\mu}(H)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}(\partial_{\rho}H^{\mu\rho}+\partial_{\rho}H^{\rho\mu}-\partial^{\mu}H_{\;\,\rho}^{\rho})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times(\partial_{\eta}H_{\mu}^{\;\,\eta}+\partial_{\eta}H_{\;\,\mu}^{\eta}-\partial_{\mu}H_{\;\,\eta}^{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}\eta_{\gamma\delta}P^{\alpha\beta,\lambda\gamma}P^{\rho\sigma,\eta\delta}\partial_{\lambda}H_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\eta}H_{\rho\sigma}.

(95)

For a discussion on the relation between the gauge-fixing Lagrangian density of unified gravity in equation (
95
) and the definition of the graviton propagator, see section
8.3
.

Next, we turn on to the Faddeev–Popov determinant part of the functional integral in equation (
93
). In analogy with the case of QED in section
7.2.1
above, the Faddeev–Popov determinants are rewritten as

\displaystyle\prod_{\mu}\det\Big(\frac{1}{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}\partial^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\!\exp\Big(-i\!\int\!\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}\partial^{2}c_{\mathrm{g}}d^{4}x\Big)\mathcal{D}c_{\mathrm{g}}\mathcal{D}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}

\displaystyle=\int\!\exp\Big(\frac{i}{\hbar c}\int\!\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,ghost}}d^{4}x\Big)\mathcal{D}c_{\mathrm{g}}\mathcal{D}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}.

(96)

In the first equality of equation (
96
), we have represented the determinant as a functional integral over anticommuting fields
c_{\mathrm{g}}=[c_{\mathrm{g}}^{0},c_{\mathrm{g}}^{x},c_{\mathrm{g}}^{y},c_{\mathrm{g}}^{z}]^{T}
and
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}=[\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{0},\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{x},\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{y},\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{z}]
belonging to the adjoint representation and called the Faddeev–Popov ghost fields of unified gravity. We have also defined
\mathcal{D}c_{\mathrm{g}}=\prod_{\mu}\mathcal{D}c_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{D}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}=\prod_{\mu}\mathcal{D}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}
. The coefficient
1/g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}
has been absorbed in the normalization of
c_{\mathrm{g}}
and
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}
. The last equality of equation (
96
) defines the Faddeev–Popov ghost Lagrangian density of unified gravity, given by

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,ghost}}=-\hbar c\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}\partial^{2}c_{\mathrm{g}}=\hbar c\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g8}}\partial^{2}c_{\mathrm{g8}}.

(97)

In the last form of equation (
97
), we have used
c_{\mathrm{g8}}=c_{\mathrm{g}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}
and
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g8}}=\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,ghost}}
in equation (
97
) does not contain the gravity gauge field,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,ghost}}
can be absorbed in the unimportant normalization constant of the functional integral in analogy with the case of QED
Peskin2018
.

7.3
Faddeev–Popov Lagrangian density of UGM and the BRST invariance

Here we conclude the Faddeev–Popov gauge fixing approach of UGM by presenting the complete locally gauge-fixed Lagrangian density of UGM. This Lagrangian density is a sum of the locally gauge-invariant Lagrangian density in equation (
66
) or (
69
) and the gauge-fixing and ghost Lagrangian densities in equations (
87
), (
89
), (
95
), and (
97
). Thus, the locally gauge-fixed Faddeev–Popov Lagrangian density of UGM is given by

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{FP}}=\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,gf}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,ghost}}.

(98)

The Faddeev–Popov Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
98
) is found to satisfy an exact global symmetry, which is known in the case of the gauge theories of the Standard Model as BRST invariance, named after Becchi, Rouet, Stara, and Tyutin
Becchi1976
;
Iofa1976
;
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
;
Fuster2005
. In the BRST method, the local electromagnetic U(1) gauge transformation parameter is defined to be proportional to the electromagnetic ghost field as
\theta=\theta^{\prime}c_{\mathrm{em}}
, where
\theta^{\prime}
is a space-time-independent anticommuting Grassmann number satisfying
\theta^{\prime 2}=0
. Correspondingly, the four U(1) transformation parameters of unified gravity are defined to be proportional to the four components of the gravitational ghost field as
\phi_{\mu}=\phi^{\prime}c_{\mathrm{g\mu}}
, where
\phi^{\prime}
is a space-time-independent anticommuting Grassmann number satisfying
\phi^{\prime 2}=0
. Then, the Faddeev–Popov Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
98
) is invariant under the following transformations associated with electromagnetism
Schwartz2014
:

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\rightarrow e^{i\theta^{\prime}c_{\mathrm{em}}Q}\psi,

(99)

\displaystyle A_{\nu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow A_{\nu}-\frac{\hbar}{e}\theta^{\prime}\vec{\partial}_{\nu}c_{\mathrm{em}},

(100)

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}ce\xi_{\mathrm{e}}}\theta^{\prime}C_{\mathrm{em}}(A),

(101)

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{em}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow c_{\mathrm{em}},

(102)

and under the following transformations associated with gravity:

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Big(\bigotimes_{\mu}e^{i\phi^{\prime}c_{\mathrm{g}(\mu)}\mathbf{t}^{(\mu)}}\Big)\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}},

(103)

\displaystyle X_{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow X_{\mu}-\frac{1}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}\phi^{\prime}c_{\mathrm{g}\mu},

(104)

\displaystyle H_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}\phi^{\prime}\vec{\partial}_{\nu}c_{\mathrm{g}\mu},

(105)

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{\kappa\hbar cg^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}\phi^{\prime}C_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}(H),

(106)

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow c_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu}.

(107)

The BRST symmetry transformations in equations (
99
)–(
107
) are global since the Grassmann numbers
\theta^{\prime}
and
\phi^{\prime}
are independent of the space-time coordinates.

In previous literature
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
, the BRST symmetry is understood to elucidate the introduction of the Faddeev–Popov ghosts. In Yang-Mills gauge theories, where ghost fields appear in virtual states, it also explains the exclusion of the ghosts from physical asymptotic states when performing quantum field theory calculations. The BRST symmetry of the path integral is known to be preserved at each loop order
Schwartz2014
. Therefore, the BRST symmetry of unified gravity, observed above, strongly suggests that unified gravity is a renormalizable gauge theory like the gauge theories of the Standard Model. This conclusion is further supported by the successful renormalization of unified gravity at 1-loop order in section
9
. The BRST symmetry of unified gravity differs from symmetries of conventional theories of gravity, where the gauge transformation generators are not constant matrices, and thus, the BRST symmetry must be replaced by a more general Batalin–Vilkovisky formalism
Batalin1981
;
Batalin1983
;
Weinberg1996
;
Costello2011
;
Henneaux1992
;
Gomis1995
.

7.4
Dynamical equations of the ghost fields

Here we investigate the dynamical equations of the ghost fields. The starting point is the Lagrangian density of the electromagnetic ghost field in equation (
89
) and that of the gravitational ghost field in equation (
97
). The Euler–Lagrange equations for
c_{\mathrm{em}}
and
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}
are given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}}{\partial c_{\mathrm{em}}}-\partial_{\rho}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}c_{\mathrm{em}})}\Big]+\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}c_{\mathrm{em}})}\Big]=0,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}}{\partial\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}}-\partial_{\rho}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}})}\Big]+\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}})}\Big]=0.

(108)

Using the Lagrangian density in equation (
89
), for the derivatives with respect to
c_{\mathrm{em}}
, we obtain
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}/\partial c_{\mathrm{em}}=0
,
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}c_{\mathrm{em}})=0
, and
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}c_{\mathrm{em}})=\hbar c\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}\eta^{\rho\sigma}
. For the derivatives with respect to
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}
, we obtain
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}/\partial\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}=\hbar c\partial^{2}c_{\mathrm{em}}
,
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}})=0
,
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,ghost}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}})=0
. Substituting these terms into equation (
108
) and dividing the equations by
\hbar c
, we obtain

\partial^{2}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\partial^{2}c_{\mathrm{em}}=0.

(109)

Similar equations are obtained for the gravitational ghost fields, written as

\partial^{2}\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\partial^{2}c_{\mathrm{g}}=0.

(110)

Equations (
109
) and (
110
) are the dynamical equations of the electromagnetic and gravitational ghost fields in UGM. The solutions for
c_{\mathrm{em}}
,
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{em}}
,
c_{\mathrm{g}}
, and
\bar{c}_{\mathrm{g}}
are arbitrary harmonic functions.

From now on, in UGM, we assume that the degrees of freedom of the electromagnetic and gravitational ghost fields have been integrated out of the path integral in equation (
80
). Accordingly, we drop out the ghost field Lagrangian densities from the considerations below. In analogy with the conventional QED, we do not need to study the dynamics of the ghost fields to investigate the dynamics of the physical fields. Since the BRST invariance is known to be preserved at each loop order
Peskin2018
, one does not either need to study the ghost fields when performing higher-order quantum field theory calculations in Abelian gauge theories, such as QED. This must extend to the theory of gravity in UGM.

7.5
Geometric Lagrangian density of UGM

Next, we present the
geometric
Lagrangian density of UGM. We call the Lagrangian density geometric after the tetrad and metric relations in equations (
78
) and (
79
) have been applied. The geometric Lagrangian density of UGM is obtained from the locally gauge-fixed Faddeev–Popov Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
98
) by dropping out the ghost field terms and by applying the Minkowski metric relation in equation (
79
). The geometric Lagrangian density of UGM is then given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{D,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,int}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,int}}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,gf}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}.

(111)

This Lagrangian density is the starting point for the derivation of the Feynman rules of UGM in section
8
. The terms of the geometric Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
111
) are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{D,kin}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hbar c}{2}\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}\vec{\partial}_{\nu}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu})\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=\bar{\Psi}\Psi

\displaystyle=\bar{\Theta}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}(\mathbf{I}_{8}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0})^{2}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}\Theta,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,int}}

\displaystyle=-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}A_{\nu}=-q_{\mathrm{e}}c\bar{\psi}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}\psi A_{\nu}=\bar{\Phi}\Theta+\bar{\Theta}\Phi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,int}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}H_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}\Big\{\frac{c}{2}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}[i\hbar\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\rho}}\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\sigma}})\psi

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-q_{\mathrm{e}}\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\rho}}A_{\sigma}+A_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\sigma}})\psi-m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}cg_{\rho\sigma}\bar{\psi}\psi]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma,\eta\lambda}\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}\partial_{\eta}A_{\lambda}\Big\}H_{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,gf}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}\xi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\partial_{\nu}A^{\nu})^{2}=-\frac{1}{\xi_{\mathrm{e}}}\bar{\Theta}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}\Theta,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}\eta_{\gamma\delta}P^{\alpha\beta,\lambda\gamma}P^{\rho\sigma,\eta\delta}\partial_{\lambda}H_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\eta}H_{\rho\sigma}.

(112)

In equation (
112
),
J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}
is the electric four-current density, given in equation (
41
), and
T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}
is the symmetric SEM tensor of the Dirac and electromagnetic fields, given in equation (
50
). Note that, in equation (
112
), the SEM tensor
T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}
is the source of gravity in analogy to how the electromagnetic four-current density
J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}
is the source of electromagnetism in QED. Correspondingly,
-(g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}/g_{\mathrm{g}})T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}H_{\mu\nu}
of equation (
112
) is the interaction term of gravity analogous to the electromagnetic interaction term
-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}A_{\nu}
of QED. With a different prefactor, the interaction term of gravity of the form
-(g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}/g_{\mathrm{g}})T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}H_{\mu\nu}
can also be found from previous literature
Berends1976
. In the sections below, we use the kinetic and gauge-fixing Lagragian density terms of the electromagnetic and gravitational fields in equation (
112
) to define the gauge-fixed kinetic Lagrangian densities of the electromagnetic and gravitational fields.

7.5.1
Gauge-fixed kinetic Lagrangian density of the electromagnetic field

We find that the Feynman gauge fixing in the eight-spinor formulation of UGM with
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1
corresponds to dropping out the term
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}^{a}\partial_{a}\Theta
of the definition of the electromagnetic spinor in equation (
17
). When this dropping is performed,
\Psi
is replaced by
\Psi^{\prime}
, given by

\Psi^{\prime}=-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\Theta.

(113)

Consequently, instead of the electromagnetic Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}=\bar{\Psi}\Psi
in equation (
112
), we obtain
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\Psi}^{\prime}\Psi^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,gf}}^{{(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}}(\partial_{\nu}A^{\nu})^{2}.

(114)

Equation equation (
114
) is the standard form of the gauge-fixed electromagnetic Lagrangian density of QED in the Feynman gauge
Peskin2018
.
The second equality of equation (
114
) shows that using
\Psi^{\prime}
provides a simple expression for the sum of the non-gauge-fixed electromagnetic Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}
and the gauge-fixing Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,gf}}^{(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}
, given by equation (
112
) for
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1
.

The generalization of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}
in equation (
114
) is given for arbitrary
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,gf}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}\xi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\partial_{\nu}A^{\nu})^{2}.

(115)

The Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}
in equation (
114
) and its generalization
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}
in equation (
115
) enable the determination of the photon propagator as discussed in section
8.2
below and in section 4 of the supplementary material.

7.5.2
Gauge-fixed kinetic Lagrangian density of the gravitational field

The gauge-fixed Lagrangian density of the gravity gauge field in UGM in the harmonic gauge with
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1
is given by the sum of the non-gauge-fixed gravity Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}
and the harmonic gauge-fixing Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}^{(\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}
, given by equation (
112
) for
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1
, as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}^{(\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\eta_{\gamma\delta}P^{\alpha\beta,\lambda\gamma}P^{\rho\sigma,\eta\delta}\partial_{\lambda}H_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\eta}H_{\rho\sigma}.

(116)

This equation is analogous to the corresponding equation of the electromagnetic field in UGM in equation (
114
).

The generalization of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}
in equation (
116
) is given for arbitrary
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,gf}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{\kappa\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}\eta_{\gamma\delta}P^{\alpha\beta,\lambda\gamma}P^{\rho\sigma,\eta\delta}\partial_{\lambda}H_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\eta}H_{\rho\sigma}.

(117)

The Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}
in equation (
116
) and its generalization
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}
in equation (
117
) enable the determination of the graviton propagator as discussed in section
8.3
below and in section 4 of the supplementary material.

7.6
Dynamical equations of UGM

The dynamics of all fields in the Lagrangian density of equation (
111
) are described by the Euler–Lagrange equations. In the sections below, we derive the dynamical equations for the gravity gauge field, the electromagnetic gauge field, and the Dirac field in UGM.

7.6.1
Field equation of gravity in UGM

Here we derive the dynamical equation of gravity in UGM. The starting point is the Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
111
) in the harmonic gauge with
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1
. The Euler–Lagrange equation for
H_{\mu\nu}
in UGM is given by

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial H_{\mu\nu}}-\partial_{\rho}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}H_{\mu\nu})}\Big]=0.

(118)

Using the Lagrangian density in equation (
111
) with
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
according to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
), we obtain
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial H_{\mu\nu}=\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,int}}/\partial H_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}
and
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}H_{\mu\nu})=-\frac{1}{\kappa}S^{\mu\nu\rho}+\frac{1}{\kappa}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}(\partial_{\lambda}H_{\sigma}^{\;\,\lambda}+\partial_{\lambda}H_{\;\,\sigma}^{\lambda}-\partial_{\sigma}H_{\;\,\lambda}^{\lambda})
. Substituting these terms into equation (
118
), multiplying the equation by
\kappa
, and rearranging the terms, we obtain

-P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\partial^{2}H_{\rho\sigma}=\kappa T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}.

(119)

This equation is the dynamical equation of the gravitational field in UGM. The source term of the gravitational field is the SEM tensor of the Dirac and electromagnetic fields in equation (
50
).

7.6.2
Maxwell’s equations in UGM

Here we derive the dynamical equation of the electromagnetic field in UGM in the eight-spinor notation. As discussed in
Partanen2024a
, the electromagnetic potential spinors
\Theta
and
\bar{\Theta}
can be treated as independent dynamical variables. The Euler–Lagrange equation for
\bar{\Theta}
is given by
Partanen2024a

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial\bar{\Theta}}-\partial_{\rho}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}\bar{\Theta})}\Big]=0.

(120)

We use the Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
111
), set
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
according to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
), and assume the Feynman gauge with
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1
to obtain
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial\bar{\Theta}=\Phi+\frac{1}{2}H_{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathbf{t}^{\mu}\Phi
and
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}\bar{\Theta})=-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}(\mathbf{I}_{8}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0})(\Psi-H_{\mu\nu}\mathbf{t}^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\Psi)-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}\Theta
. Substituting these derivatives into equation (
120
), using
\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0}\Psi=0
, equation (
17
), and the identity
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}=\mathbf{I}_{8}\partial^{2}
, and rearranging the terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial^{2}\Theta

\displaystyle=\Phi+\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}(\mathbf{I}_{8}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0})\mathbf{t}^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}(\mathbf{I}_{8}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\partial_{\rho}(H_{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}\Theta)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathbf{t}^{\mu}\Phi H_{\mu\nu}.

(121)

This equation is the eight-spinor representation of the wave equation of the electromagnetic four-potential in UGM in the presence of sources. As a consistency check, if we had started from the Euler–Lagrange equation for
\Theta
, we would have ended up to a dynamical equation that is equivalent to equation (
121
).

Next, we derive the dynamical equations of the electromagnetic field in UGM using the electromagnetic four-potential as the dynamical variable. These equations are equivalent to the eight-spinor equations above. The Euler–Lagrange equation for the electromagnetic four-potential is given by
Landau1989
;
Partanen2019b

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial A_{\sigma}}-\partial_{\rho}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma})}\Big]=0.

(122)

Using the Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
111
), setting
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
according to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
), and assuming the Feynman gauge with
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1
, we obtain
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial A_{\sigma}=-q_{\mathrm{e}}c\bar{\psi}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\sigma}\psi+q_{\mathrm{e}}cP^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\bar{\psi}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\rho}}\psi H_{\mu\nu}=-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma}+P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}J_{\mathrm{e\rho}}H_{\mu\nu}
and
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma})=-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}F^{\rho\sigma}-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\eta^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\lambda}A^{\lambda}-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma,\eta\lambda}H_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\eta}A_{\lambda}
. Substituting these terms into equation (
122
), multiplying the equation by
\mu_{0}
, and rearranging the terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial^{2}A^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mu_{0}J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\sigma}-P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma,\eta\lambda}\partial_{\rho}(H_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\eta}A_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\mu_{0}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}J_{\mathrm{e\rho}}H_{\mu\nu}.

(123)

This equation is the wave equation of the electromagnetic four-potential in UGM in the presence of sources. To the best knowledge of the authors, equation (
123
) has not been presented in previous literature. The same naturally applies to the eight-spinor form in equation (
121
).

The right-hand sides of equations (
121
) and (
123
) represent the sources of the electromagnetic field. The first terms on the right in equations (
121
) and (
123
) are associated with the well-known electron–photon vertex, the second terms on the right are associated with the photon–graviton vertex, and the third terms on the right are associated with the electron–photon–graviton vertex. The interaction vertices are discussed in more detail in section
8
.

7.6.3
Dirac equation in UGM

Next, we derive the dynamical equation of the Dirac field in UGM. As conventional, we treat
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
as independent dynamical variables. The Euler–Lagrange equation for
\bar{\psi}
is given by
Peskin2018

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial\bar{\psi}}-\partial_{\rho}\Big[\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}}{\partial(\partial_{\rho}\bar{\psi})}\Big]=0.

(124)

Using the Lagrangian density of UGM in equation (
111
) and setting
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
and
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}=m_{\mathrm{e}}
according to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
), we obtain
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial\bar{\psi}=\frac{i}{2}\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\vec{\partial}_{\rho}\psi-q_{\mathrm{e}}c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}A_{\rho}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\psi-H_{\mu\nu}[\frac{i\hbar c}{4}(\eta^{\nu\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}+\eta^{\mu\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho})\vec{\partial}_{\rho}\psi+\frac{m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\psi-\frac{q_{\mathrm{e}}c}{2}(\eta^{\nu\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}+\eta^{\mu\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho})A_{\rho}\psi]
and
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{UGM}}/\partial(\partial_{\rho}\bar{\psi})=-\frac{i}{2}\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\psi+\frac{i\hbar c}{4}H_{\mu\nu}(\eta^{\nu\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}+\eta^{\mu\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho})\psi
. Substituting these derivatives into equation (
124
), we obtain

\displaystyle i\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\vec{\partial}_{\rho}\psi\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\psi\!=q_{\mathrm{e}}c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\psi A_{\rho}

\displaystyle+P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\Big(i\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\sigma}}\vec{\partial}_{\rho}\psi-\frac{m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}}{2}\eta_{\rho\sigma}\psi+\frac{i\hbar c}{2}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\sigma}}\psi\vec{\partial}_{\rho}

\displaystyle-q_{\mathrm{e}}c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F\sigma}}\psi A_{\rho}+\frac{q_{\mathrm{e}}c}{4}\eta_{\rho\sigma}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}A_{\lambda}\psi\Big)H_{\mu\nu}.

(125)

Here we have used the definition of
P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}
in equation (
51
) and the identity
\eta^{\mu\nu}=-P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\eta_{\rho\sigma}
. Equation (
125
) is the Dirac equation in UGM. To the best knowledge of the authors, this form of the Dirac equation has not been presented in previous literature. As a consistency check, if we had started from the Euler–Lagrange equation for
\psi
, we would have ended up to a dynamical equation that is equivalent to equation (
125
).

The left-hand side of equation (
125
) is equivalent to all terms of the Dirac equation in free space. The right-hand side of equation (
125
) represents the source terms. The first term on the right in equation (
125
) corresponds to the electron–photon vertex, and the second term on the right in equation (
125
) corresponds to the electron–graviton and electron–photon–graviton vertices, which are discussed in section
8
.

8
Feynman rules and their application in UGM

Above, we have unambiguously fixed the electromagnetic and gravitational gauges. Therefore, the resulting form of unified gravity allows us to perform quantum field theory calculations using the Feynman diagrams. In this section, we formulate the Feynman rules for UGM. Instead of eight-spinors, we use the conventional Dirac spinors and the electromagnetic four-potential as dynamical variables to enable direct comparison with the Feynman rules of QED and gravity in previous literature
Schwartz2014
;
Peskin2018
;
Donoghue1994b
;
Lawrence1971
;
Butt2006
;
Ulhoa2017
;
Olyaei2018
;
Ramos2010
. For completeness, we also briefly review the derivations of the electron and photon propagators and the electron–photon vertex
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
. These derivations are presented to highlight the complete analogy with the derivations of the graviton propagator and the vertices associated with gravity, which are given below. The Feynman diagrams and rules for unified gravity in the SI units are summarized in table
2
.

Table

2
:

Feynman diagrams and the corresponding momentum-space rules for unified gravity in the SI units. The photon and graviton propagators are given in gauges defined by arbitrary gauge-fixing parameters
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}
and
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}
. Each propagator and vertex has a corresponding counterterm presented below the pertinent propagator or vertex. The red crosses at the end of external lines of vertices and counterterms indicate that these lines are not included in the expressions. In the application of the Feynman rules, the inertial and gravitational masses are set equal according to Einstein’s equivalence principle as
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}=m_{\mathrm{e}}
, and we furthermore set the coupling constant of unified gravity equal to the scale constant as
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
.

\hbox{\raise-2.56064pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_001}}}}}}}}}=\dfrac{i(\not{p}+m_{\mathrm{e}}c\mathbf{I}_{4})}{p^{2}-m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}+i\epsilon}

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{\raise-2.84544pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_002}}}}}}}}}=\dfrac{-i}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}\\
\hskip 5.69046pt\times\Big[\eta_{\mu\nu}\!-\!(1\!-\!\xi_{\mathrm{e}})\dfrac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}\Big]\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{\raise-1.42271pt\hbox{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_003}}}}}}}}}=\dfrac{i}{p^{2}\!+\!i\epsilon}\\
\times\Big[P_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}^{(D)}\!-\!\dfrac{1-\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}(\eta_{\alpha\rho}p_{\beta}p_{\sigma}\\
+\eta_{\alpha\sigma}p_{\beta}p_{\rho}+\eta_{\beta\rho}p_{\alpha}p_{\sigma}+\eta_{\beta\sigma}p_{\alpha}p_{\rho})\Big]\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_004}}}}}}}$}\\

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_006}}}}}}}$}\\

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_008}}}}}}}$}=\!\begin{array}[]{l}\\
-\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\dfrac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\\
\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\rho}(p+p^{\prime})_{\sigma}-m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta_{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\end{array}

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_009}}}}}}}$}=(Z_{\psi}-1)\dfrac{-iq_{\mathrm{e}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{0}\hbar c}}

\hskip-14.22636pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_010}}}}}}}$}=\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}\\
-\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\dfrac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}[(Z_{\mathrm{g\psi}}-1)\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\rho}(p+p^{\prime})_{\sigma}\\
-(Z_{\mathrm{g\psi}}Z_{\mathrm{g}m}-1)m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta_{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\end{array}

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_011}}}}}}}$}\!\!=-i\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\dfrac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\sigma\eta,\rho\lambda}p^{\prime}_{\eta}p_{\lambda}

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_012}}}}}}}$}\!\!=\dfrac{iq_{\mathrm{e}}}{\hbar}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa}{\varepsilon_{0}}}\dfrac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\sigma}

\hskip-8.5359pt\hbox{\raise-1.13791pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_013}}}}}}}$}}}\!\!=-i(Z_{\mathrm{g}A}-1)\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\dfrac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\sigma\eta,\rho\lambda}p^{\prime}_{\eta}p_{\lambda}

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_014}}}}}}}$}\!\!=(Z_{\mathrm{g}\psi}-1)\dfrac{iq_{\mathrm{e}}}{\hbar}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa}{\varepsilon_{0}}}\dfrac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\sigma}

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_015}}}}}}}$}=u^{s}(p)

\hbox{\raise 4.26773pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_016}}}}}}}$}}}=\bar{v}^{s}(p)

\hbox{\raise 4.26773pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_017}}}}}}}$}}}\!\!\!=\epsilon_{\mu}(p)

\hbox{\raise 4.26773pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_018}}}}}}}$}}}\!\!=\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(p)

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_019}}}}}}}$}=\bar{u}^{s}(p)

\hbox{\raise 4.26773pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_020}}}}}}}$}}}=v^{s}(p)

\hbox{\raise 4.26773pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_021}}}}}}}$}}}\!\!=\epsilon_{\mu}^{*}(p)

\hbox{\raise 4.26773pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_022}}}}}}}$}}}\!\!=\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{*}(p)

8.1
Electron propagator

To derive the electron propagator, we consider the functional integral
Peskin2018

\int e^{iS_{\mathrm{D,kin}}[\bar{\psi},\psi]/(\hbar c)}\mathcal{D}\bar{\psi}\mathcal{D}\psi.

(126)

Here
S_{\mathrm{D,kin}}[\bar{\psi},\psi]
is the action integral of the Lagrangian density of the Dirac field,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{D,kin}}
in equation (
112
). Thus, we write
Peskin2018

S_{\mathrm{D,kin}}[\bar{\psi},\psi]=\!\int\!\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{D,kin}}d^{4}x=c\!\int\!\bar{\psi}(x)(i\hbar\not{\partial}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c)\psi(x)d^{4}x.

(127)

Here we have applied partial integration to transform the integral in a form, where all derivatives apply to the Dirac spinor on the right. The electron propagator, denoted by
D_{\mathrm{D}}(x_{1}-x_{2})
, is defined to be the Green’s function of the operator
i\hbar\not{\partial}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c
, obtained from equation (
127
). Thus, it is the solution of the eigenvalue equation of this operator for a delta function source term, written as
Peskin2018

(i\hbar\not{\partial}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c)D_{\mathrm{D}}(x_{1}-x_{2})=i\mathbf{I}_{4}\delta^{4}(x_{1}-x_{2}).

(128)

Here the derivative in
\not{\partial}
operates on
x_{1}
. Applying the Fourier transform
\tilde{f}(p)\!=\!\int\!f(x)e^{ip\cdot x/\hbar}d^{4}x
with
x=x_{1}-x_{2}
to equation (
128
), we obtain

(\not{p}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\mathbf{I}_{4})D_{\mathrm{D}}(p)=i\mathbf{I}_{4}.

(129)

The solution of this equation, the momentum-space electron propagator
\tilde{D}_{\mathrm{D}}(p)
, is given by
Peskin2018

\tilde{D}_{\mathrm{D}}(p)=\dfrac{i(\not{p}+m_{\mathrm{e}}c\mathbf{I}_{4})}{p^{2}-m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}+i\epsilon}.

(130)

Here the infinitesimal constant
\epsilon
displaces the poles above and below the real axis so that
D_{\mathrm{D}}(x_{1}-x_{2})
is consistently obtained as the inverse Fourier transform of
\tilde{D}_{\mathrm{D}}(p)
.

8.2
Photon propagator

To derive the photon propagator, the Faddeev–Popov method leads us to use the gauge-fixed Lagrangian density of the electromagnetic field in the functional integral of the path integral formulation of QED
Peskin2018
. Thus, we consider the functional integral, given by

\int e^{iS_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}[A]/(\hbar c)}\mathcal{D}A.

(131)

The Feynman gauge-fixed electromagnetic action integral
S_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}[A]
in equation (
131
) is an integral of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}
in equation (
114
) over the Cartesian Minkowski coordinates, rewritten as
Peskin2018

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}[A]

\displaystyle=\!\int\!\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}d^{4}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\mu_{0}\hbar^{2}}\!\int\!A_{\mu}(x)(\eta^{\mu\nu}\hbar^{2}\partial^{2})A_{\nu}(x)d^{4}x.

(132)

Here we have applied partial integration to transform the integral in a form, where all derivatives apply to the electromagnetic four-potential on the right. The photon propagator corresponding to the applied Feynman gauge, denoted by
D^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(x_{1}-x_{2})
, is defined to be the Green’s function of the operator
\eta^{\mu\nu}\hbar^{2}\partial^{2}
, obtained from equation (
132
). Thus, it is the solution of the eigenvalue equation of this operator for a delta function source term, written as
Peskin2018

\eta^{\mu\nu}\hbar^{2}\partial^{2}D^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(x_{1}-x_{2})=i\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\delta^{4}(x_{1}-x_{2}).

(133)

Here the derivative
\partial^{2}
operates on
x_{1}
. Applying the Fourier transform,
\tilde{f}(p)\!=\!\int\!f(x)e^{ip\cdot x/\hbar}d^{4}x
with
x=x_{1}-x_{2}
, to equation (
133
), we obtain

-\eta^{\mu\nu}p^{2}\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(p)=i\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}.

(134)

The solution of this equation is the well-known momentum-space photon propagator
\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(p)
in the Feynman gauge, given by
Peskin2018

\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(p)=\frac{-i\eta_{\nu\rho}}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}.

(135)

As in the case of the electron propagator in section
8.1
, the infinitesimal constant
\epsilon
in equation (
135
) displaces the poles above and below the real axis so that
D^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(x_{1}-x_{2})
is consistently obtained as the inverse Fourier transform of
\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(em,\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}}_{\nu\rho}(p)
.

Finally, we note that the general expression of the photon propagator for an arbitrary gauge-fixing parameter
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}
is given by
Schwartz2014

\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(em)}}_{\nu\rho}(p)=\frac{-i}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}\Big[\eta_{\nu\rho}-(1-\xi_{\mathrm{e}})\frac{p_{\nu}p_{\rho}}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}\Big].

(136)

This form is obtained by using
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}
, given in equation (
115
), in place of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}
in equation (
132
).

8.3
Graviton propagator

To derive the graviton propagator, we follow the analogy with the derivation of the photon propagator in QED, discussed in section
8.2
. Correspondingly, the Faddeev–Popov method leads us to use the gauge-fixed Lagrangian density of the gravity gauge field in the functional integral of the path integral formulation of UGM. Therefore, we consider the functional integral, given by

\int e^{iS_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}[H]/(\hbar c)}\mathcal{D}H.

(137)

The harmonic gauge-fixed gravity action integral
S_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}[H]
in equation (
137
) is an integral of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}
in equation (
116
), written as

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}[H]

\displaystyle=\!\int\!\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1)}d^{4}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa\hbar^{2}}\!\int\!H_{\mu\nu}(x)(-P^{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\hbar^{2}\partial^{2})H_{\alpha\beta}(x)d^{4}x.

(138)

The graviton propagator corresponding to the applied harmonic gauge, denoted by
D^{\mathrm{(g,\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}(x_{1}-x_{2})
, is defined to be the Green’s function of the operator
-P^{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\hbar^{2}\partial^{2}
, obtained from equation (
138
). Thus, it is the solution of the eigenvalue equation of this operator for a delta function source term, written as

-P^{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\hbar^{2}\partial^{2}D^{\mathrm{(g,\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}(x_{1}-x_{2})=iI_{\rho\sigma}^{\mu\nu}\delta^{4}(x_{1}-x_{2}).

(139)

Here the derivative
\partial^{2}
operates on
x_{1}
and the identity tensor
I_{\rho\sigma}^{\mu\nu}
is defined in equation (
52
). Applying the Fourier transform,
\tilde{f}(p)\!=\!\int\!f(x)e^{ip\cdot x/\hbar}d^{4}x
with
x=x_{1}-x_{2}
, to equation (
133
), we obtain

P^{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}p^{2}\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(g,\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}(p)=iI_{\rho\sigma}^{\mu\nu}.

(140)

The solution of this equation, the momentum-space graviton propagator
\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(g,\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}(p)
, is given by

\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(g,\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}(p)=\frac{iP_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}^{(D)}}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}.

(141)

Here the infinitesimal constant
\epsilon
has the same role as discussed in the case of the electron and photon propagators above. The graviton propagator in equation (
141
) is equal to the well-known graviton propagator of linearized general relativity in the harmonic gauge
Schwartz2014
;
Donoghue1994b
;
Kibble1965
;
DeWitt1967c
.

The quantity
P_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}^{(D)}
, which we have used in writing the graviton propagator in equation (
141
), is defined as the
D
-dimensional space-time generalization of the quantity
P_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}
, given in equation (
51
). This generalization is known to be given by
Veltman1976

P_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}^{(D)}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\eta_{\mu\sigma}\eta_{\rho\nu}+\eta_{\mu\rho}\eta_{\nu\sigma}-\frac{2}{D-2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}\Big).

(142)

If we set the space-time dimension to
D=4
,
P_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}^{(D)}
in equation (
142
) becomes equivalent to
P_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}
in equation (
51
). Defining the
D
-dimensional form of the graviton propagator is essential for dimensional regularization, used in the renormalization of unified gravity in section
9
.

Finally, we note that the general expression of the graviton propagator for an arbitrary gauge-fixing parameter
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{D}^{\mathrm{(g)}}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{p^{2}\!+\!i\epsilon}\Big[P_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}^{(D)}-\frac{1-\xi_{\mathrm{g}}}{p^{2}+i\epsilon}(\eta_{\alpha\rho}p_{\beta}p_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\eta_{\alpha\sigma}p_{\beta}p_{\rho}+\eta_{\beta\rho}p_{\alpha}p_{\sigma}+\eta_{\beta\sigma}p_{\alpha}p_{\rho})\Big].

(143)

This form is obtained by using
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}
, given in equation (
117
), in place of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}^{\prime(\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1)}
in equation (
138
). The general graviton propagator in equation (
143
) can also be derived using the conventional effective field theory of gravity as shown in recent previous literature
Brandt2022
.

8.4
Electron–photon vertex

Next, we determine the electron–photon vertex. The electromagnetic interaction term of the Lagrangian density in equation (
112
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{em,int}}

\displaystyle=-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\mu}A_{\mu}=-i\sqrt{\frac{\hbar c}{\mu_{0}}}\bar{\psi}\Big(\frac{-iq_{\mathrm{e}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{0}\hbar c}}\Big)\psi A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-i\tilde{u}_{0}^{2}\tilde{A}_{0}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar c}{\mu_{0}}}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big(\frac{-iq_{\mathrm{e}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{0}\hbar c}}\Big)u(p)\epsilon_{\mu}(k).

(144)

The last form of equation (
144
) is written assuming momentum eigenstates
\psi=\tilde{u}_{0}u(p)
,
\bar{\psi}=\tilde{u}_{0}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})
, and
A_{\mu}=\tilde{A}_{0}\epsilon_{\mu}(k)
, where
\tilde{u}_{0}
and
\tilde{A}_{0}
are normalization constants and
\epsilon_{\mu}(k)
is the photon polarization vector. Then, we identify the electron–photon vertex from equation (
144
) as the term between the Dirac spinors.

8.5
Electron–graviton, photon–graviton, and electron–photon–graviton vertices

Here we use the gravitational interaction terms of the Lagrangian density in equation (
111
) to obtain vertex functions associated with the coupling of gravitons to electromagnetic and Dirac fields. The approach is analogous to how the standard electron–photon vertex is obtained from the interaction Lagrangian density of QED in section
8.4
. The gravitational interaction term
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,int}}
of the Lagrangian density in equation (
112
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,int}}=-\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}H_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\!=\!-i\sqrt{\frac{\hbar c}{\kappa}}\Big\{\!-\!\frac{i}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle\times[i\hbar\bar{\psi}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\rho}(\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\sigma})\psi-m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta_{\rho\sigma}\bar{\psi}\psi]\Big\}H_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle-\!\frac{i\hbar}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}\kappa}}\bar{\psi}\Big\{\!\frac{iq_{\mathrm{e}}}{\hbar}\sqrt{\!\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon_{0}}}\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\sigma}\Big\}\psi A_{\rho}H_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle-\!i\epsilon_{0}\hbar c\sqrt{\!\frac{c}{\kappa\hbar}}\Big\{\!\!-\!\frac{i}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\sigma\eta,\rho\lambda}\partial_{\eta}A_{\sigma}\partial_{\lambda}A_{\rho}\!\Big\}H_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\!=\!-i\tilde{u}_{0}^{2}\tilde{H}_{0}\sqrt{\frac{\hbar c}{\kappa}}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\frac{i}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\rho}(p+p^{\prime})_{\sigma}-m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta_{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(p)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(q)

\displaystyle-\!\frac{i\hbar\tilde{u}_{0}^{2}\tilde{A}_{0}\tilde{H}_{0}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}\kappa}}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big\{\!\frac{iq_{\mathrm{e}}}{\hbar}\sqrt{\!\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon_{0}}}\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\sigma}\!\Big\}u(p)\epsilon_{\rho}(k)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(q)

\displaystyle-\!\frac{i\tilde{A}_{0}^{2}\tilde{H}_{0}}{2}\sqrt{\!\frac{\epsilon_{0}^{2}c^{3}}{\kappa\hbar^{3}}}\epsilon_{\sigma}^{*}(k^{\prime})\Big\{\!\!-\!i\sqrt{\!\frac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}}{g_{\mathrm{g}}}P^{\mu\nu,\sigma\eta,\rho\lambda}k^{\prime}_{\eta}k_{\lambda}\!\Big\}

\displaystyle\times\epsilon_{\rho}(k)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(q).

(145)

In analogy with equation (
144
), the last form of equation (
145
) is written assuming momentum eigenstates
\psi=\tilde{u}_{0}u(p)
,
\bar{\psi}=\tilde{u}_{0}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})
,
A_{\rho}=\tilde{A}_{0}\epsilon_{\rho}(k)
,
A_{\sigma}=\tilde{A}_{0}\epsilon_{\sigma}^{*}(k^{\prime})
, and
H_{\mu\nu}=\tilde{H}_{0}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(q)
. Here
\tilde{H}_{0}
is the normalization constant and
\epsilon_{\mu\nu}
is the graviton polarization tensor known from previous literature
Schwartz2014
;
Jaranowski2009
. The quantities
P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}
and
P^{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma,\eta\lambda}
in equation (
145
) are given in equations (
51
) and (
53
).

In analogy with the well-known case of the electron–photon vertex in QED, discussed in section
8.4
, we identify the electron–graviton, electron–photon–graviton, and photon–graviton vertices from equation (
145
) as the terms in the wave brackets. In the application of the Feynman rules, we set
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}=m_{\mathrm{e}}
and
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
according to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
). In this work, we additionally assume the Feynman gauge of QED with
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}=1
and the harmonic gauge of unified gravity with
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}=1
. The tree-level calculations of section
8.6
show that these vertices lead to physically meaningful scattering amplitudes consistent with previous literature. The calculations at 1-loop order in section
9
show how the vertices lead to consistent renormalization of the theory. More detailed comparison of the vertices of UGM to those of the effective field theory quantization of general relativity
Donoghue1994b
;
Lawrence1971
;
Butt2006
;
Ulhoa2017
;
Olyaei2018
;
Ramos2010
is a topic of further work.

8.6
Gravitational interaction potential and scattering processes

8.6.1
Gravitational interaction potential

Here we show how the electron–graviton vertex and the graviton propagator, given in table
2
, can be used to derive the known classical limit of the gravitational interaction potential energy between two electrons. Our calculation is completely analogous to how the electron-photon vertex and the photon propagator are used to derive the classical Coulomb interaction potential energy limit of QED as summarized in section 2.10 of the supplementary material. In previous literature
Ulhoa2017
;
Santos2019
;
Santos2020
;
Ramos2010
, the gravitational scattering of two electrons is also called the gravitational Møller scattering since it is analogous to the conventional electromagnetic Møller scattering
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
;
Moller1932
;
Ashkin1954
. The gravitational Møller scattering is studied in more detail in section
8.6.2
.

In analogy with QED, there are two leading-order Feynman diagrams, and the processes are called the t- and u-channels. In the classical consideration, the effect of the indistinguishability of electrons vanishes, and thus, it is sufficient to consider the t-channel scattering. The scattering amplitude of the t-channel is given by

\displaystyle i\mathcal{M}=\hbox{\raise 1.13809pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_023}}}}}}}$}}}

\displaystyle=\!\dfrac{iP_{\alpha\mu,\beta\nu}}{(p\!-\!p^{\prime})^{2}\!+\!i\epsilon}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\rho\sigma}^{\alpha\mu,}[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p\!+\!p^{\prime})^{\sigma}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(p)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(k^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\lambda\eta}^{\beta\nu,}[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k\!+\!k^{\prime})^{\eta}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(k)

\displaystyle=\!-\frac{i\kappa c}{4\hbar}\dfrac{P_{\rho\sigma,\lambda\eta}}{(p\!-\!p^{\prime})^{2}\!+\!i\epsilon}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p\!+\!p^{\prime})^{\sigma}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]u(p)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(k^{\prime})[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+k^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]u(k).

(146)

In the last equality of equation (
146
), we have applied the identity
P_{\alpha\mu,\beta\nu}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\rho\sigma}^{\alpha\mu,}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\lambda\eta}^{\beta\nu,}=P_{\rho\sigma,\lambda\eta}
.

To derive the classical expression of the gravitational interaction potential energy, we use the nonrelativistic limit of the Dirac spinors, in which case we have

\displaystyle\bar{u}^{s^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}u^{s}(p)\rightarrow 2m_{\mathrm{e}}c\delta^{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{\rho 0}\!,\hskip 5.69046pt\bar{u}^{s^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})u^{s}(p)\rightarrow 2m_{\mathrm{e}}c\delta^{ss^{\prime}}\!\!,

\displaystyle\bar{u}^{r^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}u^{r}(k)\rightarrow 2m_{\mathrm{e}}c\delta^{rr^{\prime}}\delta^{\lambda 0}\!,\hskip 5.69046pt\bar{u}^{r^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})u^{r}(k)\rightarrow 2m_{\mathrm{e}}c\delta^{rr^{\prime}}\!\!.

(147)

Here
s
,
s^{\prime}
,
r
, and
r^{\prime}
denote spin states. As in the case of QED, discussed in section 2.10 of the supplementary material, the spin is conserved in the nonrelativistic limit. Furthermore, in the nonrelativistic limit, we have

\displaystyle(p+p^{\prime})^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=(k+k^{\prime})^{\sigma}=2m_{\mathrm{e}}c\delta^{\sigma 0}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|),

\displaystyle(p-p^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=(k^{\prime}-k)^{2}=-|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}+\mathcal{O}(|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|^{4}).

(148)

Here
\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}
is the three-dimensional momentum vector component of
p^{\prime\prime}=p-p^{\prime}=k^{\prime}-k=(p^{\prime\prime 0},\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore, in the nonrelativistic limit, the scattering amplitude in equation (
146
) becomes

\displaystyle i\mathcal{M}\!

\displaystyle=\!\frac{i\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{5}}{\hbar}\dfrac{\delta^{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{rr^{\prime}}\!P_{\rho\sigma,\lambda\eta}}{|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}-i\epsilon}(2\delta^{\rho 0}\delta^{\sigma 0}\!-\!\eta^{\rho\sigma})(2\delta^{\lambda 0}\delta^{\eta 0}\!-\!\eta^{\lambda\eta})

\displaystyle=\!\frac{2i\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{5}}{\hbar}\frac{\delta^{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{rr^{\prime}}}{|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}-i\epsilon}.

(149)

This result is compared with the Born approximation in nonrelativistic quantum mechanics, according to which the scattering amplitude is proportional to the Fourier transform
\widetilde{V}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime})
of the potential function
V(\mathbf{r})
, where
\mathbf{r}
is the position vector whose length is the distance between the particles
Peskin2018
. Accounting for the same coefficient of proportionality as in the case of deriving the Coulomb interaction potential energy of QED in section 2.10 of the supplementary material, and summing over the final spin states, we then obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar^{3}}{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c}\sum_{s^{\prime},r^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}=-\frac{\kappa\hbar^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{2(|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}-i\epsilon)}.

(150)

Then, performing the inverse Fourier transform from the momentum space to the position space gives

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{r})=\int\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime})e^{i\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}\frac{d^{3}p^{\prime\prime}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{2(2\pi)^{3}\hbar}\int\frac{e^{i\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}}{|\mathbf{p}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}-i\epsilon}d^{3}p^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{8\pi|\mathbf{r}|}e^{i|\mathbf{r}|\sqrt{i\epsilon}/\hbar}.

(151)

The intermediate steps of the calculation of this integral are given in section 7 of the supplementary material.

In the limit of
\epsilon
approaching zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we obtain the gravitational interaction potential energy from equation (
151
) as

V_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{r})=-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{8\pi|\mathbf{r}|}=-\frac{Gm_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{|\mathbf{r}|}=-\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}}{|\mathbf{r}|}.

(152)

Here
\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}
is the gravity fine structure constant, defined in equation (
76
). The negative sign of equation (
152
) is associated with the fact that the gravitational force is attractive. The gravitational interaction potential energy formula in equation (
152
) is equivalent to Newton’s law of gravitation written for two electrons. Therefore, it is the correct nonrelativistic limit of general relativity. In the case of nonzero momenta, one must account for radiative corrections. For scalar particles, such radiative corrections have been studied using the previous effective field theory quantization of gravity in
Donoghue1994a
;
BjerrumBohr2003
;
Faller2008
;
Netto2022
. In our case of UGM, the radiative corrections to the Newtonian potential are studied in section
10.2
.

8.6.2
Gravitational Møller scattering

Next, we investigate the momentum dependence of the gravitational Møller scattering in the nonrelativistic limit. Accounting for both the t- and u-channel scattering processes, in analogy to the electromagnetic Møller scattering
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
, the total scattering amplitude of the gravitational Møller scattering is given by

\displaystyle i\mathcal{M}=\hbox{\raise 1.13809pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_024}}}}}}}$}}}\;\;+\;\hbox{\raise-1.70717pt\hbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[]{feynman_025}}}}}}}$}}}

\displaystyle=\!\dfrac{iP_{\alpha\mu,\beta\nu}}{(p\!-\!p^{\prime})^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\rho\sigma}^{\alpha\mu,}[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p\!+\!p^{\prime})^{\sigma}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(p)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(k^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\lambda\eta}^{\beta\nu,}[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k\!+\!k^{\prime})^{\eta}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(k)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\!\dfrac{iP_{\alpha\mu,\beta\nu}}{(p\!-\!k^{\prime})^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(k^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\rho\sigma}^{\alpha\mu,}[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p\!+\!k^{\prime})^{\sigma}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(p)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big\{\!-\!\dfrac{i}{2}\sqrt{\dfrac{\kappa c}{\hbar}}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\lambda\eta}^{\beta\nu,}[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k\!+\!p^{\prime})^{\eta}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\Big\}u(k)

\displaystyle=\!-\frac{i\kappa c}{4\hbar}P_{\rho\sigma,\lambda\eta}\Big\{\dfrac{1}{t}\bar{u}(p^{\prime})[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p\!+\!p^{\prime})^{\sigma}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]u(p)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(k^{\prime})[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+k^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]u(k)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\dfrac{1}{u}\bar{u}(k^{\prime})[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p\!+\!k^{\prime})^{\sigma}\!-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]u(p)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\!\bar{u}(p^{\prime})[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+p^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]u(k)\Big\}.

(153)

In the last equality of equation (
153
), we have applied the identity
P_{\alpha\mu,\beta\nu}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\rho\sigma}^{\alpha\mu,}P_{\;\;\;\;\;\lambda\eta}^{\beta\nu,}=P_{\rho\sigma,\lambda\eta}
.
We have also used the Mandelstam variables, defined as
Mandelstam1958
;
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=(p+k)^{2}=(p^{\prime}+k^{\prime})^{2}=E_{\mathrm{cm}}^{2}/c^{2},

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=(p-p^{\prime})^{2}=(k^{\prime}-k)^{2}=-2p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}(1-\cos\theta_{\mathrm{r}}),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=(p-k^{\prime})^{2}=(p^{\prime}-k)^{2}=-2p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}(1+\cos\theta_{\mathrm{r}}).

(154)

Here
E_{\mathrm{cm}}=2E_{\mathrm{r}}
is the total energy of the electrons in the center of mass frame. The last forms of the Mandelstam variables in equation (
154
) correspond to the parametrization of the four-momenta of the incoming and outgoing electrons, given by

\displaystyle p^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(E_{\mathrm{r}}/c,0,0,p_{\mathrm{r}}),

\displaystyle k^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(E_{\mathrm{r}}/c,0,0,-p_{\mathrm{r}}),

\displaystyle p^{\prime\mu}

\displaystyle=(E_{\mathrm{r}}/c,p_{\mathrm{r}}\sin\theta_{\mathrm{r}},0,p_{\mathrm{r}}\cos\theta_{\mathrm{r}}),

\displaystyle k^{\prime\mu}

\displaystyle=(E_{\mathrm{r}}/c,-p_{\mathrm{r}}\sin\theta_{\mathrm{r}},0,-p_{\mathrm{r}}\cos\theta_{\mathrm{r}}).

(155)

Using equation (
153
), we obtain the square of the scattering amplitude as

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}c^{2}}{16\hbar^{2}}P_{\rho\sigma,\lambda\eta}P_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\Big(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\{[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p+p^{\prime})^{\sigma}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\alpha}(p+p^{\prime})^{\beta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\mathrm{Tr}\{[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+k^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\gamma}(k+k^{\prime})^{\delta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\gamma\delta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\frac{1}{u^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\{[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p+k^{\prime})^{\sigma}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\alpha}(p+k^{\prime})^{\beta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\mathrm{Tr}\{[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+p^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\gamma}(k+p^{\prime})^{\delta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\gamma\delta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{1}{tu}\mathrm{Tr}\{[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p+p^{\prime})^{\sigma}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\alpha}(p+k^{\prime})^{\beta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+k^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\gamma}(k+p^{\prime})^{\delta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\gamma\delta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{1}{tu}\mathrm{Tr}\{[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(p+k^{\prime})^{\sigma}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\rho\sigma}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\alpha}(p+p^{\prime})^{\beta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\alpha\beta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{p^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\lambda}(k+p^{\prime})^{\eta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\lambda\eta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times[\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\gamma}(k+k^{\prime})^{\delta}-m_{\mathrm{e}}c\eta^{\gamma\delta}\mathbf{I}_{4}]\mathbf{u}_{k^{\prime}}\}\Big).

(156)

In equation (
156
), we have expressed the terms in terms of traces and applied the cyclicity of the trace to write all dependencies on the Dirac spinors using the shorthand notation
\mathbf{u}_{p}=u(p)\bar{u}(p)
.

To calculate the unpolarized scattering cross section, we average
|\mathcal{M}|^{2}
in equation (
156
) over the initial spins, sum over the final spins, apply the completeness relations of the Dirac spinors, given by
\sum_{s}\mathbf{u}_{p}^{s}=\sum_{s}u^{s}(p)\bar{u}^{s}(p)=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}p_{\rho}+m_{\mathrm{e}}c\mathbf{I}_{4}

Peskin2018
, and finally use the trace relations of the Dirac gamma matrices
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
to obtain

\displaystyle\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{s,s^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}}|\mathcal{M}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}c^{2}}{64\hbar^{2}t^{2}u^{2}}\{(s-t)^{2}t^{2}(5s^{2}-6st+5t^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-tu(-9s^{4}+12s^{3}t-4st^{3}+t^{4})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+u^{2}(5s^{4}-12s^{3}t+8s^{2}t^{2}+4st^{3}-3t^{4})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+2u^{3}(-8s^{3}+2st^{2}+t^{3})+u^{4}(22s^{2}+4st-3t^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-u^{5}(16s+t)+5u^{6}+4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}[-2s^{3}(t^{2}+7tu+u^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-(t+u)(t^{2}+u^{2})(2t^{2}-7tu+2u^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+s^{2}(t+u)(2t^{2}+31tu+2u^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+2s(t^{4}-12t^{3}u-13t^{2}u^{2}-12tu^{3}+u^{4})]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-16m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{4}[-s(2t-3u)(3t-2u)(t+u)

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+(t^{2}+tu+u^{2})(3s^{2}+3t^{2}-11tu+3u^{2})]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-64m_{\mathrm{e}}^{6}c^{6}tu[5(t+u)-8s]+256m_{\mathrm{e}}^{8}c^{8}(t^{2}-tu+u^{2})\}.

(157)

Using the last forms of the Mandelstam variables in equation (
154
) and the equation for the unpolarized scattering cross section of particles with equal masses in the center-of-mass frame in terms of
\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle

Schwartz2014
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{d\sigma}{d\Omega}\Big)_{\mathrm{cm}}=\frac{\hbar^{2}c^{2}}{64\pi^{2}E_{\mathrm{cm}}^{2}}\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}}{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{2}E_{\mathrm{cm}}^{2}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{4}\sin^{4}\theta_{\mathrm{r}}}\Big[16(m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{4}+8m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}+8p_{\mathrm{r}}^{4})^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-4(3m_{\mathrm{e}}^{8}c^{8}\!+\!53m_{\mathrm{e}}^{6}c^{6}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}\!+\!270m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{4}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{4}\!+\!456m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{6}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+240p_{\mathrm{r}}^{8})\sin^{2}\theta_{\mathrm{r}}+p_{\mathrm{r}}^{4}(61m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{4}\!+\!240m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\!186p_{\mathrm{r}}^{4})\sin^{4}\theta_{\mathrm{r}}-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{8}\sin^{6}\theta_{\mathrm{r}}\Big].

(158)

Equation (
158
) is the scattering cross section of the unpolarized gravitational Møller scattering in unified gravity. In the nonrelativistic limit,
p_{\mathrm{r}}\ll m_{\mathrm{e}}c
, the unpolarized gravitational Møller scattering cross section in equation (
158
) becomes

\Big(\frac{d\sigma}{d\Omega}\Big)_{\mathrm{cm}}=\frac{\hbar^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{4}c^{6}}{E_{\mathrm{cm}}^{2}p_{\mathrm{r}}^{4}\sin^{4}\theta_{\mathrm{r}}}(4-3\sin^{2}\theta_{\mathrm{r}}).

(159)

In comparison with the nonrelativistic limit of the unpolarized electromagnetic Møller scattering cross section, summarized in section 2.11 of the supplementary material, the only difference of equation (
159
) is that the gravity fine structure constant appears in place of the electromagnetic fine structure constant. This is as expected in the nonrelativistic limit. The same result follows from the somewhat different scattering amplitudes calculated in previous works
Ramos2010
. For larger momenta, in analogy with the electromagnetic Møller scattering, one must account for the radiative corrections. This is left as a topic of further work.

9
Renormalization of unified gravity

9.1
Introduction to renormalization of quantum gravity

To obtain finite values for physical quantities in quantum field theories, one often needs to apply the renormalization procedure
Schwartz2014
;
Peskin2018
. This procedure removes infinities that arise in the description of self-energies and higher-order scattering processes involving loops in the Feynman diagrams. The general complexity of this procedure is compounded by the need to renormalize different types of interactions and to account for the perturbative and nonperturbative regimes of the theories. Further subtleties follow from possible spontaneous symmetry breaking, anomalies, and nonrenormalizable interactions. Despite these challenges, renormalization is essential in making quantum field theories physically predictive and mathematically consistent.

In the case of gravity, it is known that general relativity does not fit the paradigm of describing fundamental interactions by renormalizable quantum field theories
Schwartz2014
;
Hooft1974
;
Deser1974a
;
Deser1974b
;
Deser1974c
;
Goroff1985
. This is because the gravitational interactions in general relativity are of such a form that the induced divergences cannot be absorbed by the redefinition of the parameters of the theory. Therefore, general relativity is considered inherently nonrenormalizable
Schwartz2014
;
Zee2010
;
Maggiore2005
. In the conventional effective-field-theory-based quantization of gravity, this problem is circumvented by introducing an infinite series of new coupling constants and higher-order gravitational terms in the Lagrangian density to absorb the divergences. The infinite number of free parameters, however, ruins the predictivity of the theory. Thus, only the leading low-energy, long-distance quantum corrections can be reliably calculated using the effective-field-theory-based quantum gravity
Donoghue1994a
;
Donoghue1994b
.

The goal of renormalizing quantum gravity is to develop a consistent theory unifying the principles of general relativity with quantum mechanics. Many previous approaches, such as string theory
Becker2007
;
Dine2007
;
Green1987
and loop quantum gravity
Ashtekar1986
;
Jacobson1988
;
Rovelli1990
;
Rovelli2008
, have been developed but they have not led to the ultimate success. Thus, a complete and widely accepted renormalizable theory of quantum gravity remains unknown.

Below, we present a restricted investigation of the renormalizability of unified gravity. We aim at showing that, because of the different gauge symmetries, the renormalizability properties of unified gravity are fundamentally different from those in the conventional nonrenormalizable effective field theory of gravity. We prove that the theory is renormalizable at 1-loop order and leave the complete proof of renormalizability to all loop orders as a topic of further work. In comparison, conventional gravity is well known to be nonrenormalizable already at 1-loop order
Hooft1974
;
Deser1974a
;
Deser1974b
;
Deser1974c
.

9.2
Renormalization of unified gravity in the UGM formulation

In this section, we study the renormalization of unified gravity in the Minkowski metric (UGM) at 1-loop order. As in previous sections, we limit our study to the system of the Dirac electron–positron field, the electromagnetic field, and the gravitational field. The renormalized theory is expressed in terms of the renormalization factors, to be presented and discussed in section
9.3
.

In this work, we use the on-shell renormalization scheme
Schwartz2014
;
Peskin2018
, also known as the pole scheme or the physical scheme. The on-shell scheme is especially suitable for particles that can travel over asymptotically large distances, such as electrons, photons, and gravitons, studied in this work. The on-shell scheme also has an advantage that its mass parameters straightforwardly correspond to the physical masses and no separate corrections of the parameters are needed
Schwartz2014
.

We present the relevant Feynman diagrams and calculate all 1-loop contributions to the on-shell renormalization factors of the theory. The calculations are found to be analogous to those of the renormalization of the gauge theories of the Standard Model
Schwartz2014
. The radiative corrections of selected physical quantities are calculated in section
10
. Here we use the unscaled representation of unified gravity and QED, instead of the scaled representations discussed in section
6.4
above and in section 2.9 of the supplementary material. The 1-loop contributions to the on-shell renormalization factors of UGM using the conventional dimensional regularization are collected from the sections below and summarized in table
3
.

The electromagnetic part of the renormalization factors follows from electromagnetic interactions through the electron–photon and electron–photon–graviton vertices. The gravitational part follows from gravitational interactions through the electron–graviton, photon–graviton, and electron–photon–graviton vertices.

9.3
Dressed states and renormalization factors

We start our study of the renormalization of unified gravity with the discussion of the bare and dressed states and the renormalization factors. The renormalization is based on recognizing that the fields and physical constants in the classical Lagrangian density, in the absence of quantum-field-theoretical interactions, are bare quantities. In the presence of interactions, the bare particles, like electrons, interact through the gauge fields, leading to the creation of particle-antiparticle pairs, virtual photons, and other excitations that modify the bare states to become dressed states. Thus, a dressed state refers to a quantum state that is the fundamental excitation of the theory in the presence of interactions. Therefore, the bare quantities neglect the quantum-field-theoretical virtual particle corrections associated with the Feynman loop diagrams. The bare fields and the bare physical constants are typically infinite and written in terms of the renormalized quantities as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}\psi_{\mathrm{bare}}&\!\!=&\sqrt{Z_{\psi}}\psi,&A_{\mathrm{bare}}^{\mu}&\!\!=&\sqrt{Z_{A}}A^{\mu},\\[5.0pt]
m_{\mathrm{e,bare}}&\!\!=&Z_{m}m_{\mathrm{e}},&e_{\mathrm{bare}}&\!\!=&Z_{e}e,\\[5.0pt]
\xi_{\mathrm{e,bare}}&\!\!=&Z_{\mathrm{\xi e}}\xi_{\mathrm{e}},&H_{\mathrm{bare}}^{\mu\nu}&\!\!=&\sqrt{Z_{H}}H^{\mu\nu},\\[5.0pt]
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e,bare}}&\!\!=&Z_{\mathrm{g}m}m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}},&g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g,bare}}&\!\!=&Z_{\mathrm{g}}g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}},\\[5.0pt]
\xi_{\mathrm{g,bare}}&\!\!=&Z_{\mathrm{\xi g}}\xi_{\mathrm{g}}.\end{array}

(160)

The renormalization relations for
\psi
,
A^{\mu}
,
m_{\mathrm{e}}
,
e
and
\xi_{\mathrm{e}}
in equation (
160
) are well known in QED
Schwartz2014
;
Peskin2018
. In unified gravity, these relations are complemented with the renormalization of the gravity gauge field
H^{\mu\nu}
, the gravitational mass
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}
, the gravity coupling constant
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}
, and the gravity gauge-fixing parameter
\xi_{\mathrm{g}}
.

That the gravitational and inertial masses are associated with different renormalization factors in equation (
160
) does not mean that the gravitational and inertial masses are not equal. According to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
), we assume that the renormalized values of these masses are equal. Together with the generally different renormalization factors, the equivalence principle thus means that the bare values of the gravitational and inertial masses are generally different. This is not in conflict with any experiments since the bare masses have anyway infinite values and they are not experimental observables.

For convenience, we define the scaled renormalization factors
Z_{e\psi}
,
Z_{\mathrm{g\psi}}
, and
Z_{\mathrm{g}A}
by the following relations:

Z_{e}=\frac{Z_{e\psi}}{Z_{\psi}\sqrt{Z_{A}}},\hskip 14.22636ptZ_{\mathrm{g}}=\frac{Z_{\mathrm{g\psi}}}{Z_{\psi}\sqrt{Z_{H}}}=\frac{Z_{\mathrm{g}A}}{Z_{A}\sqrt{Z_{H}}}.

(161)

As is well known, the electromagnetic gauge invariance of QED implies via the Ward–Takahashi identity that the renormalization factors
Z_{e\psi}
and
Z_{\psi}
are equal as
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

Z_{e\psi}=Z_{\psi}.

(162)

The violation of this equality would be an indication of a gauge anomaly breaking the gauge symmetry of QED. Consistent theories, such as the Standard Model, are known to be free from gauge anomalies
Schwartz2014
. In the sections below, we show at 1-loop order that equation (
162
) is satisfied in unified gravity in the presence of gravitational interactions. This derivation provides one strong argument for the consistency of unified gravity.

Furthermore, the renormalization factors of the gauge-fixing parameters are set equal to the renormalization factors of the gauge fields as

Z_{\mathrm{\xi e}}=Z_{A},\hskip 14.22636ptZ_{\mathrm{\xi g}}=Z_{H}.

(163)

These equations imply that the gauge-fixing Lagrangian densities do not obtain counterterms as shown in section
9.4
below.

In perturbation theory, the renormalization factors
Z_{i}
are expanded in powers of the expansion parameter
\lambda
according to

Z_{i}=1+\lambda\delta Z_{i}^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}\delta Z_{i}^{(2)}+\ldots\;.

(164)

Accordingly, at 1-loop order, we expand
Z_{i}
up to the first power in
\lambda
and set
\lambda\rightarrow 1
. The higher-order terms in
\lambda
are dropped out. Order by order, the renormalization factor terms,
