). In the limit of
\epsilon
approaching zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{e,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{e^{2}}{8\pi^{2}\varepsilon_{0}r}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\!\Big[\frac{2\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{3}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}\!\frac{e^{p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}}\sqrt{1\!-\!\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}}\Big(1\!+\!\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)dp_{\mathrm{ri}}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+\!\frac{2\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{3}\!\!\int_{2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{\infty}\!\frac{e^{-p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}}\sqrt{1\!-\!\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}}\Big(1\!+\!\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)dp_{\mathrm{ri}}\Big]

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{2\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{3\pi r}\!\!\int_{2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}}\sqrt{1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}}\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)dp_{\mathrm{ri}}

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{2\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{3\pi r}\!\!\int_{2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\sqrt{(p_{\mathrm{ri}}-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c)(p_{\mathrm{ri}}+2m_{\mathrm{e}}c)}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)dp_{\mathrm{ri}}.

(280)

While taking the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we have used
p_{\mathrm{r}}=\epsilon+ip_{\mathrm{ri}}
and
p_{\mathrm{r}}=-\epsilon+ip_{\mathrm{ri}}
to change the integration variables of the first and second integrals, respectively. In the last two equalitites of equation (
280
), we have made a change of variables,
p_{\mathrm{ri}}\rightarrow-p_{\mathrm{ri}}
, to the first integral and used some algebra to obtain the last form.

Next, we evaluate the integral in the last form of equation (
280
) in the long distance limit of
r\gg 1/(m_{\mathrm{e}}c)
. In this limit, the integral is dominated by the region where
p_{\mathrm{ri}}\approx 2m_{\mathrm{e}}c
. Approximating the integrand in this region and changing the variable to
p_{\mathrm{t}}=p_{\mathrm{ri}}-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c
, we obtain

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{e,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{\pi r}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-(p_{\mathrm{t}}+2m_{\mathrm{e}}c)r/\hbar}}{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}\sqrt{4m_{\mathrm{e}}cp_{\mathrm{t}}}dp_{\mathrm{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}cr/\hbar}}{2\pi r(m_{\mathrm{e}}c)^{3/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-p_{\mathrm{t}}r/\hbar}\sqrt{p_{\mathrm{t}}}dp_{\mathrm{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}cr/\hbar}}{2\pi r(m_{\mathrm{e}}c)^{3/2}}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2(r/\hbar)^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{4\sqrt{\pi}|\mathbf{r}|(m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar)^{3/2}}.

(281)

This radiative correction of the Coulomb potential is known from QED, where the classical Coulomb potential corrected with this term is known as the Uehling potential
Peskin2018
;
Landau1982
.

10.1.2
Renormalized 1-graviton-loop contribution

Next, we calculate the 1-graviton-loop correction to the Coulomb potential. Using the renormalized 1-graviton-loop contribution to the Fourier transform of the Coulomb potential correction in equation (
276
) together with equation (
274
), and performing the inverse Fourier transform, we obtain the renormalized 1-graviton-loop contribution to the Coulomb potential correction as

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{e,1L,graviton}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\int\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{e,1L,graviton}}(\mathbf{p})e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}\kappa c^{2}}{48\pi^{4}\hbar}\int\frac{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}\log(|\mathbf{p}|^{2})}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}-i\epsilon}e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}d^{3}p

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}\kappa\hbar^{2}c^{2}}{12\pi^{2}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}e^{i|\mathbf{r}|\sqrt{i\epsilon}/\hbar}.

(282)

In the third equality, we have applied the second Fourier transform integral given in section 7 of the supplementary material.

In the limit of
\epsilon
approaching zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, the renormalized 1-graviton-loop contribution of the Coulomb potential correction in equation (
282
) becomes

V_{\mathrm{e,1L,graviton}}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{\kappa\hbar^{2}c^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{12\pi^{2}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}=\frac{2G\hbar^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{3\pi c^{2}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}.

(283)

This result corresponds to the conventional effective-field-theory-based gravity quantization result for the vacuum polarization term of the Coulomb potential correction, which can be found from some works in previous literature
Butt2006
.

10.1.3
Total vacuum-polarization-corrected Coulomb potential

The sum of the classical Coulomb potential and the 1-electron-loop and 1-graviton-loop vacuum polarization corrections to the Coulomb potential is given by

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{e}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{e}}(\mathbf{r})+V_{\mathrm{e,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})+V_{\mathrm{e,1L,graviton}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{|\mathbf{r}|}+\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{4\sqrt{\pi}|\mathbf{r}|(m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar)^{3/2}}+\frac{2G\hbar^{2}\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{3\pi c^{2}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar c\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{|\mathbf{r}|}\Big[1+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{4\sqrt{\pi}(m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar)^{3/2}}+\frac{2G\hbar}{3\pi c^{3}|\mathbf{r}|^{2}}\Big].

(284)

The Uehling potential of QED corresponds to the first two terms of this equation
Schwartz2014
;
Peskin2018
;
Landau1982
, and the last term is the modification of the QED result by the gravitational interaction.

10.2
Radiative corrections to the Newtonian potential

Next, we study the radiative corrections to the Newtonian potential. As an example, we calculate the 1-electron-loop and 1-photon-loop corrections resulting from the vacuum polarization diagrams and their counterterm in equations (
190
)–(
192
). Detailed study of the contributions resulting from the other relevant Feynman diagrams, partly similar to those studied in
Donoghue1994a
, is left to a separate work.

In the nonrelativistic limit, for the four-momentum of the exchanged graviton, we have
p^{2}\rightarrow-|\mathbf{p}|^{2}
, where
\mathbf{p}
is the three-dimensional momentum vector. Thus, the 1-electron-loop and 1-photon-loop renormalized scalar amplitude factors of the graviton self-energy in equations (
209
) and (
210
) become

\displaystyle\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(p^{2})\approx\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-|\mathbf{p}|^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa c|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}{32\pi^{2}\hbar}\Big\{\frac{8m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{3|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\Big(1-\frac{6m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\Big)-\log(m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\Big(1-\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times\log\Big[1-\frac{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}\Big(\sqrt{1+\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}}-1\Big)\Big]\Big\},

(285)

\displaystyle\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,photon}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(p^{2})

\displaystyle\approx\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,photon}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-|\mathbf{p}|^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa c|\mathbf{p}|^{2}}{16\pi^{2}\hbar}\log(|\mathbf{p}|^{2}).

(286)

The Fourier transform of the Newtonian potential obtains corrections, which are additions to the tree-level Fourier transform of the Newtonian potential in equation (
150
). Using equations (
285
) and (
286
), the renormalized 1-electron-loop and 1-photon-loop correction terms are given by

\tilde{V}_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{p})=-\frac{\kappa\hbar^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{2(|\mathbf{p}|^{2}-i\epsilon)}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-|\mathbf{p}|^{2}),

(287)

\tilde{V}_{\mathrm{g,1L,photon}}(\mathbf{p})=-\frac{\kappa\hbar^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{2(|\mathbf{p}|^{2}-i\epsilon)}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,photon}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-|\mathbf{p}|^{2}).

(288)

10.2.1
Renormalized 1-electron-loop contribution

Here we study the renormalized 1-electron-loop contribution to the radiative correction of the Newtonian potential. Using the renormalized 1-electron-loop contribution to the Fourier transform of the Newtonian potential correction in equation (
287
), and performing the inverse Fourier transform, we obtain the renormalized 1-electron-loop contribution to the Newtonian potential correction as

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle\!=\!\int\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{p})e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}

\displaystyle\!=\!-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{8\pi^{2}r}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!\frac{ip_{\mathrm{r}}(e^{-ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}\!\!-\!e^{ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar})}{p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})dp_{\mathrm{r}}.

(289)

The intermediate steps in obtaining the last form of equation (
289
) are analogous to those in equation (
277
).

As a complex function, the imaginary part of
\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})
in the integrand of equation (
289
) has branch cuts at the positive and negative imaginary axes starting at
p_{\mathrm{r}}=\pm 2im_{\mathrm{e}}c
and extending to infinities. Writing
p_{\mathrm{r}}=\pm\epsilon+ip_{\mathrm{ri}}
, where
p_{\mathrm{ri}}=\mathrm{Im}(p_{\mathrm{r}})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}[\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})]

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\kappa cp_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}{32\pi\hbar}\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}.

(290)

The positive and negative signs of equation (
290
) correspond to
\mathrm{Re}(p_{\mathrm{r}})>0
and
\mathrm{Re}(p_{\mathrm{r}})<0
, respectively.

Applying the residue theorem and the branch cuts of the integrand to calculate the integral of equation (
289
), we obtain

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle\!=\!-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{8\pi^{2}r}\Big\{\!\!-\!2\pi i\!\!\!\underset{p_{\mathrm{r}}=-\sqrt{i\epsilon}}{\mathrm{Res}}\Big[ip_{\mathrm{r}}\frac{e^{-ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt-\!2\pi i\!\!\underset{p_{\mathrm{r}}=\sqrt{i\epsilon}}{\mathrm{Res}}\Big[ip_{\mathrm{r}}\frac{e^{ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt-2\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon-2im_{\mathrm{e}}c}\frac{ip_{\mathrm{r}}e^{-ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}}{(p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon)}i\mathrm{Im}[\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})]dp_{\mathrm{r}}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+2\int_{-\epsilon+2im_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}\frac{ip_{\mathrm{r}}e^{ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}}{(p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon)}i\mathrm{Im}[\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2})]dp_{\mathrm{r}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\!=\!-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{8\pi^{2}r}\Big\{-2\pi i\Big[\frac{1}{2}e^{ir\sqrt{i\epsilon}/\hbar}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-i\epsilon)\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt-2\pi i\Big[\frac{1}{2}e^{ir\sqrt{i\epsilon}/\hbar}\Xi_{\mathrm{1L,electron}}^{\mathrm{(r)}}(-i\epsilon)\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+\!\frac{\kappa c}{16\pi\hbar}\!\!\int_{\epsilon-i\infty}^{\epsilon-2im_{\mathrm{e}}c}\!\frac{p_{\mathrm{r}}^{3}e^{-ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}}{(p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{r}}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+\!\frac{\kappa c}{16\pi\hbar}\!\!\int_{-\epsilon+2im_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{-\epsilon+i\infty}\!\frac{p_{\mathrm{r}}^{3}e^{ip_{\mathrm{r}}r/\hbar}}{(p_{\mathrm{r}}^{2}-i\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{r}}\Big\}.

(291)

In the second equality of equation (
291
), we have calculated the residues and applied equation (
290
). In the limit of
\epsilon
approaching zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})=-\frac{\kappa m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{4}}{8\pi^{2}r}\!\Big[\frac{\kappa c}{16\pi\hbar}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}\!p_{\mathrm{ri}}e^{p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{ri}}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+\!\frac{\kappa c}{16\pi\hbar}\!\!\int_{2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{\infty}\!p_{\mathrm{ri}}e^{-p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{ri}}\Big]

\displaystyle\!=\!-\frac{\kappa^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}}{64\pi^{3}\hbar r}\!\!\int_{2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{\infty}\!p_{\mathrm{ri}}e^{-p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{ri}}

\displaystyle\!=\!-\frac{\kappa^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}}{64\pi^{3}\hbar r}\!\!\int_{2m_{\mathrm{e}}c}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-p_{\mathrm{ri}}r/\hbar}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}[(p_{\mathrm{ri}}-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c)(p_{\mathrm{ri}}+2m_{\mathrm{e}}c)]^{3/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt\times\Big(1+\frac{2m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}{p_{\mathrm{ri}}^{2}}\Big)dp_{\mathrm{ri}}.

(292)

While taking the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we have used
p_{\mathrm{r}}=\epsilon+ip_{\mathrm{ri}}
and
p_{\mathrm{r}}=-\epsilon+ip_{\mathrm{ri}}
to change the integration variables of the first and second integrals, respectively. In the last two equalitites of equation (
292
), we have made a change of variables,
p_{\mathrm{ri}}\rightarrow-p_{\mathrm{ri}}
, to the first integral and used some algebra to obtain the last form.

Next, we evaluate the integral in the last form of equation (
292
) in the long distance limit of
r\gg 1/(m_{\mathrm{e}}c)
. In this limit, the integral is dominated by the region where
p_{\mathrm{ri}}\approx 2m_{\mathrm{e}}c
. Approximating the integrand in this region and changing the variable to
p_{\mathrm{t}}=p_{\mathrm{ri}}-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c
, we obtain

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{3\kappa^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}}{128\pi^{3}\hbar r}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-(p_{\mathrm{t}}+2m_{\mathrm{e}}c)r/\hbar}}{4m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}(4m_{\mathrm{e}}cp_{\mathrm{t}})^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{t}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\kappa^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}cr/\hbar}}{64\pi^{3}\hbar\sqrt{m_{\mathrm{e}}c}\,r}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-p_{\mathrm{t}}r/\hbar}p_{\mathrm{t}}^{3/2}dp_{\mathrm{t}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\kappa^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}cr/\hbar}}{64\pi^{3}\hbar\sqrt{m_{\mathrm{e}}c}\,r}\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}}{4(r/\hbar)^{5/2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{9\kappa^{2}c^{3}(m_{\mathrm{e}}c\hbar)^{3/2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{256\pi^{2}\sqrt{\pi}\,|\mathbf{r}|^{7/2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{9G^{2}(m_{\mathrm{e}}c\hbar)^{3/2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{4\sqrt{\pi}c^{5}|\mathbf{r}|^{7/2}}.

(293)

This is the 1-electron-loop contribution to the radiative correction of the Newtonian potential in unified gravity. The exponential factor of equation (
293
) is equivalent to that in the QED contribution to the radiative correction of the Coulomb potential in equation (
281
) but, otherwise, these corrections depend on different powers of
|\mathbf{r}|
. The authors are not aware of the appearance of the correction term in equation (
293
) in previous literature. Previously, arguments have been presented in favor of omitting all the diagrams with internal lines of massive particles in the calculations of quantum gravity corrections
Donoghue1994a
;
Netto2022
. However, these arguments have been constructed for scalar particles, and thus, they do not apply to electron loops.

10.2.2
Renormalized 1-photon-loop contribution

Next, we calculate the 1-photon-loop correction to the Newtonian potential. Using the renormalized 1-photon-loop contribution to the Fourier transform of the Newtonian potential correction in equation (
288
) together with equation (
286
), and performing the inverse Fourier transform, we obtain the renormalized 1-photon-loop contribution to the Newtonian potential correction as

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g,1L,photon}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\int\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{g,1L,photon}}(\mathbf{p})e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}}{256\pi^{5}\hbar^{2}}\int\frac{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}\log(|\mathbf{p}|^{2})}{|\mathbf{p}|^{2}-i\epsilon}e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{r}/\hbar}d^{3}p

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa^{2}\hbar m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}}{64\pi^{3}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}e^{i|\mathbf{r}|\sqrt{i\epsilon}/\hbar}.

(294)

In the third equality, we have applied the second Fourier transform integral given in section 7 of the supplementary material.

In the limit of
\epsilon
approaching zero as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, the renormalized 1-photon-loop contribution of the Newtonian potential correction in equation (
294
) becomes

V_{\mathrm{g,1L,photon}}(\mathbf{r})=-\frac{\kappa^{2}\hbar m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{5}}{64\pi^{3}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}=-\frac{G^{2}\hbar m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{\pi c^{3}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}.

(295)

Apart from the prefactor, the form of this term is identical to the form of the quantum correction term of the Newtonian potential obtained using the conventional effective field theory in previous literature, where other Feynman diagrams were also accounted for
Donoghue1994a
.

10.2.3
Total vacuum-polarization-corrected Newtonian potential

The sum of the classical Newtonian potential and the 1-electron-loop and 1-photon-loop vacuum polarization corrections to the Newtonian potential is given by

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathbf{r})+V_{\mathrm{g,1L,photon}}(\mathbf{r})+V_{\mathrm{g,1L,electron}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=-\frac{Gm_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{|\mathbf{r}|}-\frac{G^{2}\hbar m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{\pi c^{3}|\mathbf{r}|^{3}}-\frac{9G^{2}(m_{\mathrm{e}}c\hbar)^{3/2}e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{4\sqrt{\pi}c^{5}|\mathbf{r}|^{7/2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{Gm_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}}{|\mathbf{r}|}\Big(1+\frac{G\hbar}{\pi c^{3}|\mathbf{r}|^{2}}+\frac{9G\hbar e^{-2m_{\mathrm{e}}c|\mathbf{r}|/\hbar}}{4c^{3}\sqrt{\pi m_{\mathrm{e}}c/\hbar}|\mathbf{r}|^{5/2}}\Big).

(296)

10.3
Radiative corrections to the electron magnetic moment

The form factor
F_{2}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}
in the electron–photon vertex matrix element in equation (
218
) is called the anomalous magnetic moment since the total magnetic moment of an electron is given by
g_{\mathrm{s}}=2+2F_{2}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}

Schwartz2014
. Order by order in perturbation theory, we obtain more and more accurate approximation for
F_{2}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}
. Experiments have verfied the Standard Model prediction for the electron magnetic moment to an astonishing accuracy of one part in a trillion
Fan2023
. More experimental accuracy is, however, needed to probe the corrections associated with quantum gravity.

The 1-photon-loop radiative correction of the form factor
F_{2}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}
following from equation (
213
) is the famous result first calculated by Schwinger
Schwinger1948
, given by

F_{\mathrm{2,1L,photon}}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}=\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{e}}}{2\pi}.

(297)

Correspondingly, the 1-graviton-loop radiative corrections of the form factor
F_{2}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}
following from equation (
214
) is given by

F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{e\gamma}}=\frac{7\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}}{4\pi}.

(298)

The 1-graviton-loop contributions to equation (
298
) following from each Feynman diagram in equation (
214
) are given by

F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{(e\gamma,diag1)}}=-\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}}{6\pi}\Big[\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{UV}}}+\frac{61}{6}+\log\!\Big(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}e^{-\gamma}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}\Big)\Big],

(299)

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{(e\gamma,diag2)}}=F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{(e\gamma,diag3)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}}{\pi}\Big[\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{UV}}}+\frac{7}{2}+\log\!\Big(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}e^{-\gamma}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}\Big)\Big],

(300)

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{(e\gamma,diag4)}}=F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{(e\gamma,diag5)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{33\alpha_{\mathrm{g}}}{36\pi}\Big[\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\mathrm{UV}}}+\frac{64}{33}+\log\!\Big(\frac{4\pi\mu^{2}e^{-\gamma}}{m_{\mathrm{e}}^{2}c^{2}}\Big)\Big],

(301)

F_{\mathrm{2,1L,graviton}}^{\mathrm{(e\gamma,diag6)}}=0.

(302)

Equation (
298
) agrees with the result first calculated by Berends and Gastmans
Berends1975
. Since the counterterm does not contribute to equation (
298
), this result was possible to calculate without the renormalizable theory of quantum gravity presented in this work. The exact cancellation of the UV divergences and the scale and Euler–Mascheroni constants of dimensional regularization in the terms of equations (
299
)–(
301
) when substituted into equation (
298
) is necessary for physical observables to be finite and independent of the regularization parameters. In previous literature, another method for the calculation of radiative corrections of the electron magnetic moment has been based on dimensional reduction of supergravity
delAguila1984
;
Grisaru1985
;
Georges1985
;
Ottoni2006
. The results of this approach differ from our results.

11
Obtaining TEGR from unified gravity

In this section, we show that by an appropriate choice of the tetrad and the metric tensor, we obtain the theory of teleparallel equivalent of general relativity in the Weitzenböck gauge (TEGRW)
Bahamonde2023a
;
Aldrovandi2012
;
Heisenberg2019
;
Jimenez2019
;
Maluf2013
;
Cabral2020
;
Blagojevic2013
directly from unified gravity presented above. The geometric conditions of this section can be called the Weitzenböck gauge fixing. The Weitzenböck gauge fixing does not fix the gauge unambiguously since there are remaining redundant degrees of freedom associated with the class of reference frames where the teleparallel spin connection vanishes
Bahamonde2023a
;
Aldrovandi2012
.

11.1
Geometric conditions of TEGRW: tetrad and metric tensor

The form of the gauge-invariant Lagrangian density of unified gravity in equation (
69
) explicitly depends on the gauge field
H_{a\nu}
in analogy to how the Lagrangian density explicitly depends on the electromagnetic gauge field
A_{\nu}
when the electromagnetic-gauge-covariant derivatives have been written out using equation (
13
). In contrast to the gauge theories of the Standard Model, in the case of the gauge theory of gravity, it is possible to absorb the explicit dependence of the Lagrangian density on the gauge field into the definition of the tetrad. This possibility is enabled by the equivalence principle in equation (
37
). Using the equivalence principle of scale,
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{g}}=g_{\mathrm{g}}
, with the reduced form of the Lagrangian density of unified gravity in equation (
69
), we observe that the explicit dependence on
H_{a\nu}
can be absorbed in the generalized tetrad
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}
, defined as

\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}=\partial_{\nu}x_{a}+H_{a\nu}.

(303)

Equations (
34
) and (
303
) and the resulting definition of the metric tensor below are called in this work
the geometric conditions of TEGRW
. The first term of
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}
in equation (
303
) is related to inertial effects, and the second term describes gravitational effects. The division of the tetrad into
\partial_{\nu}x_{a}
and
H_{a\nu}
in equation (
303
) reminds to how one divides the tetrad into trivial and nontrivial parts in TEGRW in previous literature
Aldrovandi2012
.

Even if the expression of the space-time-dependent tetrad in equation (
303
) is qualitatively of the same form as that in the conventional representation of TEGRW
Bahamonde2023a
;
Aldrovandi2012
, in the present theory, it has been introduced in a fundamentally different way. In the conventional gauge theory approach to TEGRW, the gauge field is the translation gauge field, which is added to make the expression of the tetrad invariant in translations of the tangent-space coordinates
x_{a}
. In contrast, in the present theory, equation (
303
) is introduced as a geometric condition that hides the explicit local gauge invariance of the Lagrangian density with respect to the gauge symmetry transformations of
X_{a}
and
H_{a\nu}
in equations (
44
) and (
59
).

Having defined the space-time-dependent tetrad in equation (
303
), we then write the general space-time-dependent metric tensor in the conventional way in terms of the tetrad as

g_{\mu\nu}=\eta^{ab}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\mu}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{b\nu}.

(304)

As a consistency check, when the gauge field
H_{a\nu}
is zero, the definition of the tetrad in equation (
303
) and the metric tensor in equation (
304
) straightforwardly simplify to the well-known representations of the tetrad and the metric tensor in flat space-time in the chosen coordinates.

11.2
Geometric Lagrangian density of TEGRW

Next, we present the
geometric
Lagrangian density of TEGRW obtained from unified gravity. We call the Lagrangian density geometric after the geometric condition of TEGRW in equation (
303
) has been applied to the reduced form of the locally gauge-invariant Lagrangian density of unified gravity in equation (
69
). We also present different forms of this geometric Lagrangian density. Using the definition of the tetrad in the Weitzenböck gauge fixing in equation (
303
) and setting
m^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}=m_{\mathrm{e}}
according to the equivalence principle in equation (
37
), the Lagrangian density of unified gravity in equation (
69
) becomes the Lagrangian density of TEGRW, given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\!\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}\!\Big[\frac{i\hbar c}{4}\bar{\psi}_{8}(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathbf{t}^{a}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{F}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathbf{t}^{a}\vec{D})\psi_{8}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}}{2}\bar{\psi}_{8}\mathbf{t}^{a}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\psi_{8}-\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{t}^{a}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\Psi\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}_{8}\psi_{8}+\bar{\Psi}\Psi+\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{a\mu\nu}S^{a\mu\nu}\Big\}\sqrt{-g}.

(305)

Using the identities
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathbf{t}^{a}\vec{D}=2\vec{D}
,

\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathbf{t}^{a}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}=2\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}
,
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}\bar{\psi}_{8}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\bar{\mathbf{t}}^{a}\psi_{8}=-4\bar{\psi}\psi
,
\bar{\psi}_{8}\psi_{8}=-\bar{\psi}\psi
,
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\bar{\mathbf{t}}^{a}\Psi=\mathbf{0}
,
\bar{\Psi}\Psi=\bar{\Theta}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}(\mathbf{I}_{8}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0})^{2}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}\Theta
, and
H_{a\mu\nu}S^{a\mu\nu}=H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}
, and writing out the electromagnetic-gauge-covariant derivatives using equation (
23
), the Lagrangian density in equation (
305
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}

\displaystyle\!=\!\Big[\frac{i\hbar c}{2}\bar{\psi}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{F}}\vec{\partial}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}})\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi+\bar{\Phi}\Theta+\bar{\Theta}\Phi

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\bar{\Theta}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}(\mathbf{I}_{8}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0})^{2}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\sigma}\vec{\partial}_{\sigma}\Theta

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}\Big]\sqrt{-g}.

(306)

Here the electromagnetic interaction Lagrangian density
\bar{\Phi}\Theta+\bar{\Theta}\Phi
is written using the charge-current spinor
\Phi
, given in equation (
19
). The form of the Lagrangian density of TEGRW in equation (
306
) is used in the derivation of Maxwell’s equations of TEGRW in the eight-spinor formalism, discussed in section
11.3.2
.

Using
\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{F}}\vec{\partial}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}\vec{\partial}_{\nu}
,
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}=\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}
,
\bar{\Phi}\Theta+\bar{\Theta}\Phi=-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}A_{\nu}=-q_{\mathrm{e}}c\bar{\psi}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}\psi A_{\nu}
, and rewriting the electromagnetic Lagrangian density in terms of the electromagnetic field strength tensor
F^{\mu\nu}
using equation (
24
), the Lagrangian density of TEGRW in equation (
306
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}

\displaystyle\!=\!\Big[\frac{i\hbar c}{2}\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}\vec{\partial}_{\nu}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu})\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}A_{\nu}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}\Big]\sqrt{-g}.

(307)

This form of the Lagrangian density of TEGRW is used in the derivations of the Maxwell’s equations in TEGRW in the four-vector and tensor formalism, discussed in section
11.3.2
, and Dirac equation of TEGRW, discussed in section
11.3.3
. Apart from the last term of the Lagrangian density in equation (
307
) describing the gravity gauge field, equation (
307
) is directly seen to be equal to the Lagrangian density of QED, discussed in section 2.8 of the supplementary material.

Writing the Lagrangian density in terms of the tetrad and the inverse metric, and using again the electromagnetic-gauge-covariant derivatives instead of the explicit electromagnetic interaction term
-J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}A_{\nu}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}

\displaystyle=\!\Big\{\frac{\!i\hbar c}{4}\bar{\psi}[\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{b}^{\;\,\nu}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{b}\vec{D}_{\nu}\!+\!\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\nu}\vec{D}^{b})\!-\!(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}_{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{b}\!+\!\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}^{b}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}\nu})\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{b}^{\;\,\nu}]\psi

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\!m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi\!-\!\frac{1}{4\mu_{0}}g^{\mu\rho}g^{\nu\sigma}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}\!+\!\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}\Big\}\sqrt{-g}.

(308)

This form of the Lagrangian density of TEGRW is used as the starting point of the derivation of the field equation of gravity in TEGRW, discussed in section
11.3.1
. The Lagrangian density in equation (
308
) is found
to be equivalent
to the Lagrangian density of TEGRW in previous literature
Aldrovandi2012
. Detailed comparison of equation (
308
) to the Lagrangian density of TEGRW in previous literature, including also references to pertinent equations of a well-known textbook of TEGR, is presented in section 6 of the supplementary material. The relation to the Lagrangian density of general relativity is also discussed in section 6 of the supplementary material. Further comparison of unified gravity with previous theories of gravity can be found in section
13.2
.

11.3
Dynamical equations of TEGRW

The dynamics of all fields appearing in the Lagrangian density in equation (
66
) are described by the well-known Euler–Lagrange equations. In the sections below, we derive the dynamical equations for the gravity gauge field, the electromagnetic gauge field, and the Dirac field in TEGRW.

11.3.1
Field equation of gravity in TEGRW

Next, we present the dynamical equation of the gravity gauge field, which we call the field equation of gravity in TEGRW. Starting from the Euler–Lagrange equation for
H_{a\nu}
, after some algebra, presented in section 5.1 of the supplementary material, we obtain the field equation of gravity in TEGRW, given by

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}S^{a\nu\rho}=\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\rho}S^{a\nu\rho}=\kappa T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}.

(309)

The source term
T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}=\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\mu}^{a}T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}
in equation (
309
) is the total SEM tensor of the Dirac, electromagnetic, and gravitational fields in TEGRW, given by

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=T_{\mathrm{D}}^{\mu\nu}+T_{\mathrm{D,diff}}^{\mu\nu}+T_{\mathrm{em}}^{\mu\nu}+T_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{D}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{i\hbar c}{4}\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}\vec{D}^{\nu}+\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu}\vec{D}^{\mu}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}^{\nu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\mu}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}^{\mu}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\nu})\psi

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\Big[\frac{i\hbar c}{2}\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\vec{D}_{\rho}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho})\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi\Big],

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{D,diff}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\Big[\frac{i\hbar c}{2}\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\vec{D}_{\rho}-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$D$}}$}_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho})\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi\Big],

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{em}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\Big(F_{\;\;\rho}^{\mu}F^{\rho\nu}+\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}\Big),

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa}\Big(H^{\;\,\;\;\mu}_{\sigma\rho}S^{\sigma\rho\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}H_{\rho\sigma\lambda}S^{\rho\sigma\lambda}\Big).

(310)

The terms
T_{\mathrm{D}}^{\mu\nu}
and
T_{\mathrm{em}}^{\mu\nu}
of equation (
310
) are the SEM tensors of the Dirac and electromagnetic fields, equal to those in equation (
50
). The term
T_{\mathrm{D,diff}}^{\mu\nu}
of equation (
310
) is an additional Dirac field SEM tensor contribution that is obtained from the Euler–Lagrange equation of gravity in TEGRW. As shown in section 5.4 of the supplementary material, this term is, however, zero when the Dirac equation of TEGRW is satisfied, i.e., for on-shell states. The term
T_{\mathrm{g}}^{\mu\nu}
of equation (
310
) is the SEM tensor of the gravitational gauge field, which is also known from previous literature on TEGR
Aldrovandi2012
;
Bahamonde2023a
;
Andrade2000
. Similarly to the SEM tensor of the electromagnetic field, the SEM tensor of the gravitational field is traceless. Thus, the associated force carriers, the gravitons, are massless. The trace of the total SEM tensor in equation (
310
), given by
{T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}}_{\;\,\nu}^{\nu}=m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi
, is then equal to the trace of the SEM tensor of QED, given in equation (
57
).

By operating both sides of equation (
309
) by
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}
, the left-hand side becomes
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}S^{a\nu\rho}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}(\sqrt{-g}\,S^{a\nu\rho})=0
since
\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}
is symmetric and
S^{a\nu\rho}
is antisymmetric with respect to the indices
\nu
and
\rho
. Therefore, the total SEM tensor
T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}
is conserved as

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}=\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\nu}T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}=0.

(311)

This conservation law is necessary for the consistency of the theory
Bahamonde2023a
.

As shown in section 5.1 of the supplementary material, the field equation of TEGRW in equation (
309
) can also be written in a form showing only space-time indices as

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}S^{\mu\nu\rho}=\kappa\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}.

(312)

On the right-hand side of equation (
312
), the SEM pseudotensor
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}
is given by

\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}=T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{\kappa}S^{\sigma\rho\nu}\mathbin{\mathop{\Gamma}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}^{{\mkern-1.0mu\raise-3.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu$}}}_{\;\,\sigma\rho}.

(313)

By operating both sides of equation (
312
) by
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}
, the left-hand side becomes
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}S^{\mu\nu\rho}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}(\sqrt{-g}\,S^{\mu\nu\rho})=0
since
\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}
is symmetric and
S^{\mu\nu\rho}
is antisymmetric with respect to the indices
\nu
and
\rho
. Therefore, the total SEM pseudotensor
\mathfrak{T}^{\mu\nu}
is conserved as

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}=0.

(314)

Due to the pseudotensor nature of
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}
, the conservation law of
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}
in equation (
314
) cannot be written in terms of the Levi-Civita coordinate-covariant derivative in the same way as the conservation laws of
T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}
and
T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}
in equations (
56
) and (
311
).

11.3.2
Maxwell’s equations in TEGRW

Next, we present the dynamical equation of the electromagnetic field in TEGRW. In the eight-spinor formalism, the dynamical equation of the electromagnetic field is obtained from the Euler–Lagrange equation for
\Theta
or
\bar{\Theta}

Partanen2024a
. After some algebra, presented in section 5.2 of the supplementary material, we obtain the spinorial Maxwell equation, given by

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}\Psi)=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}(\vec{\partial}_{\rho}+\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho}^{a}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma})\Psi=-\Phi.

(315)

Equation (
315
) corresponds to the spinorial Maxwell equation, introduced in
Partanen2024a
, generalized to include coupling to gravity. The coupling to gravity comes through
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}
. In flat space-time, we have
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}=0
. In Cartesian coordinates, this relation is trivial, but it also holds in curvilinear coordinate systems, such as in spherical coordinates. Therefore, the spinorial Maxwell equation in general coordinates in flat space-time can be written as
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\rho}\vec{\partial}_{\rho}\Psi=-\Phi
. The only change to the Cartesian Minkowski space-time form studied in
Partanen2024a
is the use of general coordinates.

The derivation of the spinorial Maxwell equation in equation (
315
) is equivalent but different from the conventional electromagnetic four-potential-based derivation of Maxwell’s equations. Starting from the Euler–Lagrange equation for
A_{\nu}
and using also the Bianchi identity, after some algebra, presented in section 5.2 of the supplementary material, we obtain the conventional form of Maxwell’s equations in general relativity as a set of two equations, given by
Misner1973

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}F^{\rho\nu}=\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\rho}F^{\rho\nu}=\mu_{0}J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu},

(316)

\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\rho}F_{\mu\nu}+\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\mu}F_{\nu\rho}+\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\nu}F_{\rho\mu}=0.

(317)

As shown in
Partanen2024a
, the spinorial Maxwell equation, i.e., equation (
315
), is equivalent to the full set of conventional Maxwell’s equations. In contrast, in the four-vector and tensor formalism, one obtains only Gauss’s law for electricity and the Ampére–Maxwell law, i.e., equation (
316
), directly from the Euler–Lagrange equations. Gauss’s law for magnetism and Faraday’s law, i.e., equation (
317
), are obtained by using the Bianchi identity of the electromagnetic field tensor
Misner1973
.

By operating both sides of equation (
316
) by
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}
, the left-hand side becomes
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\rho}F^{\rho\nu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}(\sqrt{-g}\,F^{\rho\nu})=0
since
\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}
is symmetric and
F^{\rho\nu}
is antisymmetric with respect to the indices
\nu
and
\rho
. The right-hand side becomes
\mu_{0}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}=\mu_{0}\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\nu}J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}
. Therefore, the electromagnetic four-current density is conserved as

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\nu}J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}=\mathbin{\mathop{\nabla}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\nu}J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}=0.

(318)

Another derivation of the conservation law in equation (
318
), based on the U(1) symmetry of QED, is given in section 2.4 of the supplementary material. In the case of electromagnetism in TEGRW, the two derivations lead to the same conservation law of the electric four-current density
J_{\mathrm{e}}^{\nu}
, whereas, in the case of gravity in TEGRW, the derivations lead to conservation laws of different objects: the form
T_{\mathrm{m}}^{\mu\nu}
of the SEM tensor of the Dirac and electromagnetic fields in equation (
56
) and the form
T_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{a\nu}
of the total SEM tensor including the gravitational field in equation (
311
). Furthermore, in the case of gravity in TEGRW, we have the conservation law of the SEM pseudotensor
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathrm{TEGRW}}^{\mu\nu}
, given in equation (
314
).

11.3.3
Dirac equation in TEGRW

Next, we present the dynamical equation of the Dirac field in TEGRW. Starting from the Euler–Lagrange equation for
\psi
or
\bar{\psi}
, after some algebra, presented in section 5.3 of the supplementary material, we obtain the dynamical equation of the Dirac field, given by

i\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\Big(\vec{D}_{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho}^{a}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}\Big)\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\psi=0.

(319)

Equation (
319
) is the Dirac equation in TEGRW. We note that, in flat space-time, we have
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}=0
. Therefore, the Dirac equation in general coordinates in flat space-time simplifies to its conventional form, given by
i\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}\vec{D}_{\rho}\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\psi=0
, as expected. Comparison of equations (
315
) and (
319
) shows that the quantity that multiplies the term
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho}^{a}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}
is the spin since it is
S=1
for vector bosons and
S=\frac{1}{2}
for Dirac fermions.

The Dirac equation of TEGRW in equation (
319
) can be compared with previous formulations of the Dirac equation in TEGRW. We write the term
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho}^{a}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}
in equation (
319
) as
\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho}^{a}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}=\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho}^{a}\partial_{\sigma}\mathbin{\mathop{e}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{a}^{\;\,\sigma}+\mathbin{\mathop{\Gamma}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\circ$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho\sigma}^{{\mkern-1.0mu\raise-3.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma$}}}=-\mathbin{\mathop{K}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\rho\sigma}^{{\mkern-1.0mu\raise-3.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma$}}}=-\mathbin{\mathop{T}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\sigma\rho}^{{\mkern-1.0mu\raise-3.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma$}}}=-\mathcal{V}_{\rho}
. In the first equality, we have applied partial differentiation. In the second equality, we have applied equation (
6
). In the third equality, we have applied the definition of the contortion tensor in equation (
9
) and the antisymmetry of the torsion tensor in its last two indices. In the last equality, we have defined
\mathcal{V}_{\rho}=\mathbin{\mathop{T}\limits^{\vbox to0.0pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}\vss}}}_{\;\,\sigma\rho}^{{\mkern-1.0mu\raise-3.01389pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sigma$}}}
, which is the vector part of the torsion decomposition
Aldrovandi2012
. Thus, the Dirac equation in equation (
319
) becomes
i\hbar c\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{\rho}(\vec{D}_{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{V}_{\rho})\psi-m_{\mathrm{e}}c^{2}\psi=0
. In the absence of the electromagnetic coupling, we set
\vec{D}_{\rho}=\vec{\partial}_{\rho}
, in which case, the Dirac equation above corresponds the Dirac equation in equation (12.36) of
Aldrovandi2012
for zero spin connection.

12
Unified gravity and the full Standard Model

In this section, our goal is to explain how unified gravity is extended to cover all quantum fields of the full Standard Model. We start with a review of the representations of quantum fields of different particle types. For the quantum fields of the Standard Model, we present two different but mathematically equivalent representations. The first one of these representations is the standard representation, and the second one is the eight-spinor representation, which was originally introduced in the case of the electromagnetic field in
Partanen2024a
. Finally, we include the fields of all particle types in the Lagrangian density, and thus, unify gravity with the Lagrangian density of the Standard Model.

12.1
Fermions

12.1.1
Standard representation

The Standard Model fermions have spin
S=\frac{1}{2}
. They are divided into quarks and leptons. The quark fields are described by the conventional Dirac spinors
q^{i}
for SU(3) color charge
i\in\{r,g,b\}
and flavor
q\in\{u,d,c,s,t,b\}
. For a given flavor, we write
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

q=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}q^{r}\\
q^{g}\\
q^{b}\end{array}\right].

(320)

Using this notation, the color charge index of quark fields is not shown in the equations below.

There are three generations of SU(2) doublet pairs of left-handed quarks and leptons, indexed by
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, as
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{L}}^{i}

\displaystyle\in\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}u_{\mathrm{L}}\\
d_{\mathrm{L}}\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{c}c_{\mathrm{L}}\\
s_{\mathrm{L}}\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{c}t_{\mathrm{L}}\\
b_{\mathrm{L}}\end{array}\right]\right\},

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{L}}^{i}

\displaystyle\in\left\{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\nu_{e\mathrm{L}}\\
e_{\mathrm{L}}\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\nu_{\mu\mathrm{L}}\\
\mu_{\mathrm{L}}\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\nu_{\tau\mathrm{L}}\\
\tau_{\mathrm{L}}\end{array}\right]\right\},

and there are three generations of SU(2) singlet right-handed quarks and leptons, indexed by
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, as
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

u_{\mathrm{R}}^{i}\in\{u_{\mathrm{R}},c_{\mathrm{R}},t_{\mathrm{R}}\},\hskip 14.22636ptd_{\mathrm{R}}^{i}\in\{d_{\mathrm{R}},s_{\mathrm{R}},b_{\mathrm{R}}\},

(333)

e_{\mathrm{R}}^{i}\in\{e_{\mathrm{R}},\mu_{\mathrm{R}},\tau_{\mathrm{R}}\},\hskip 14.22636pt\nu_{\mathrm{R}}^{i}\in\{\nu_{e\mathrm{R}},\nu_{\mu\mathrm{R}},\nu_{\tau\mathrm{R}}\}.

(334)

For simplicity, we introduce a notation combining the quark and lepton fields, indexed by
j\in\{1,2,...,6\}
, as

\psi_{j}^{i}\in\{Q_{\mathrm{L}}^{i},u_{\mathrm{R}}^{i},d_{\mathrm{R}}^{i},L_{\mathrm{L}}^{i},\nu_{\mathrm{R}}^{i},e_{\mathrm{R}}^{i}\}.

(335)

12.1.2
Eight-spinor representation

The eight-spinor representation of fermions is obtained from the standard representation above in a simple way generalizing equation (
18
) as

\psi_{8j}^{i}=\psi_{j}^{i}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}=[\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\psi_{j}^{i},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}]^{T}.

(336)

Again, the transpose in equation (
336
) only operates on the eight-spinor degree of freedom. For a discussion of selected aspects of Dirac eight-spinors, see section
2.4
.

12.2
Vector bosons

The vector bosons of the Standard Model, having spin
S=1
, are the force carriers of the fundamental interactions for elementary fermions. They are introduced as gauge fields through the gauge-covariant derivative acting on fermion fields. Therefore, vector bosons are often called gauge bosons. In the absence of vector bosons, the Lagrangian density of the Standard Model satisfies the symmetries U(1), SU(2), and SU(3) globally, i.e., for constant, space-time-independent, symmetry transformation parameters. These global symmetries are then promoted to local symmetries, with space-time-dependent symmetry transformation parameters, by introducing the gauge-covariant derivative and utilizing gauge theory. To describe dynamics of the gauge fields, the kinetic field strength terms of each gauge field are also added in the Lagrangian density.

12.2.1
Standard representation

The gauge-covariant derivative, through which the vector bosons of the Standard Model have been introduced, is given by
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbf{D}}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\vec{\partial}_{\nu}-i\frac{g_{\mathrm{s}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}G_{l\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}}{2}-i\frac{g_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}W_{i\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}}{2}-i\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}B_{\nu}\frac{Y_{\mathrm{w}}}{2},

\displaystyle\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}_{\nu}+i\frac{g_{\mathrm{s}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}G_{l\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}}{2}+i\frac{g_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}W_{i\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}}{2}+i\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}B_{\nu}\frac{Y_{\mathrm{w}}}{2}.

(337)

Here
G_{la}
, with
l\in\{1,2,...,8\}
, is the SU(3) gauge field of eight gluons, and
g_{\mathrm{s}}
is the associated coupling constant of strong interaction. The SU(3) generators are the Gell-Mann matrices
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}/2
, which act on the SU(3) color charge degrees of freedom in equation (
320
). The quantity
W_{i\nu}
, with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, is the SU(2) gauge field associated with weak isospin and coupling constant
g_{\mathrm{ew}}
. The canonically normalized SU(2) generators
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}/2
are given by the Pauli matrices, which act on the SU(2) doublet degrees of freedom in equation (
12.1.1
) and in the Higgs field described below. The quantity
B_{\nu}
is the U(1)
Y
gauge field associated with weak hypercharge
Y_{\mathrm{w}}
and coupling constant
g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ew}}
. In the matrix form, the gauge fields
\mathbf{G}_{\nu}
and
\mathbf{W}_{\nu}
of the strong and weak interactions are written as
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

\mathbf{G}_{\nu}=G_{l\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}}{2},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbf{W}_{\nu}=W_{i\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}}{2}

(338)

The commutator of the gauge-covariant derivative defines the corresponding field strength tensors
\mathbf{G}_{\mu\nu}
and
\mathbf{W}_{\mu\nu}
, for which we obtain
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{G}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{G}_{\mu}-ig_{\mathrm{s}}[\mathbf{G}_{\mu},\mathbf{G}_{\nu}]=G_{l\mu\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}}{2},

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046ptG_{l\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}G_{l\nu}-\partial_{\nu}G_{l\mu}+g_{\mathrm{s}}(f_{\mathrm{s}})_{l}^{\;\,mn}G_{m\mu}G_{n\nu},

(339)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{W}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{W}_{\mu}-ig_{\mathrm{ew}}[\mathbf{W}_{\mu},\mathbf{W}_{\nu}]=W_{i\mu\nu}\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}}{2},

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046ptW_{i\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}W_{i\nu}-\partial_{\nu}W_{i\mu}+g_{\mathrm{ew}}(f_{\mathrm{w}})_{i}^{\;\,jk}W_{j\mu}W_{k\nu}.

(340)

Here
(f_{\mathrm{s}})_{l}^{\;\,mn}=-\frac{i}{4}\mathrm{Tr}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}[\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{m},\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{n}])
, with
l,m,n\in\{1,2,...,8\}
, are the totally antisymmetric structure constants of the strong interaction, and
(f_{\mathrm{w}})_{i}^{\;\,jk}=-\frac{i}{4}\mathrm{Tr}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}[\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{j},\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{k}])=\varepsilon^{ijk}
, with
i,j,k\in\{1,2,3\}
, are the corresponding structure constants of the weak interaction.

The diagonal Lie algebra metric is determined to lower and raise the Lie algebra indices. For strong interaction, the Lie algebra metric is
(\eta_{\mathrm{s}})^{mn}=\delta^{mn}
. Correspondingly, for weak interaction, we have
(\eta_{\mathrm{w}})^{jk}=\delta^{jk}
. Therefore, the Lie algebra indices of the strong and weak interactions can be written lowered or raised without paying attention to it.

The electroweak unification is based on the symmetry breaking of SU(2)
\otimes
U(1)
{}_{\mathrm{Y}}\rightarrow
U(1)
em

Schwartz2014
;
Glashow1961
;
Weinberg1967
;
Salam1968
. The high-energy
\mathrm{U(1)}_{\mathrm{Y}}
is not to be confused with the low-energy U(1)
em
. The U(1)
em
symmetry of QED is generated by a linear combination the weak hypercharge and the third SU(2) generator of the weak isospin, i.e.,
Q=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{3}+Y_{\mathrm{w}}\mathbf{I}_{2})
. The neutral W
0
boson of the three vector bosons of the weak isospin (W
+
, W
-
, W
0
) mixes with the weak hypercharge gauge boson (B). This results in the observed Z
0
intermediate vector boson and the photon. Thus, there are three intermediate vector bosons (W
+
, W
-
, Z
0
) and one photon.

12.2.2
Eight-spinor representation

In analogy to how the electromagnetic spinor in equation (
17
) is formed from the Cartesian Minkowski coordinate form of the electromagnetic field strength tensor
F_{ab}
in equation (
2.4
)
Partanen2024a
, we form eight gluon spinors
\mathcal{G}_{l}
from the gluon field strength tensor
G_{lab}
, one for each
l
, three intermediate vector boson spinors
\mathcal{W}_{i}
from the field strength tensor
W_{iab}
, one for each
i
, and one weak hypercharge field spinor
\mathcal{B}
from the field strength tensor
B_{ab}
as

\mathcal{G}_{l}=[0,G_{l0x},G_{l0y},G_{l0z},0,iG_{lzy},iG_{lxz},iG_{lyx}]^{T},

(341)

\mathcal{W}_{i}=[0,W_{i0x},W_{i0y},W_{i0z},0,iW_{izy},iW_{ixz},iW_{iyx}]^{T},

(342)

\mathcal{B}=[0,B_{0x},B_{0y},B_{0z},0,iB_{zy},iB_{xz},iB_{yx}]^{T}.

(343)

For simplicity, we introduce a notation combining the different eight-spinor gauge field strengths, indexed by
i\in\{1,2,...,12\}
, as

\Psi_{i}\in\{\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2},...,\mathcal{G}_{8},\mathcal{W}_{1},\mathcal{W}_{2},\mathcal{W}_{3},\mathcal{B}\}.

(344)

In analogy to the electromagnetic potential spinor in equation (
16
)
Partanen2024a
, we form the gluon potential spinors
G_{l}
, the intermediate vector boson potential spinors
W_{i}
, and the weak hypercharge potential spinor
B
. These spinors are four-vector-type eight-spinors
Partanen2024a
, formed from the components of
G_{l}^{\;\,a}
,
W_{i}^{\;\,a}
, and
B^{a}
as

\displaystyle G_{l}

\displaystyle=[0,G_{l}^{\;\,x},G_{l}^{\;\,y},G_{l}^{\;\,z},G_{l}^{\;\,0},0,0,0]^{T}

\displaystyle=[0,-G_{lx},-G_{ly},-G_{lz},G_{l0},0,0,0]^{T},

(345)

\displaystyle W_{i}

\displaystyle=[0,W_{i}^{\;\,x},W_{i}^{\;\,y},W_{i}^{\;\,z},W_{i}^{\;\,0},0,0,0]^{T}

\displaystyle=[0,-W_{ix},-W_{iy},-W_{iz},W_{i0},0,0,0]^{T},

(346)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=[0,B^{x},B^{y},B^{z},B^{0},0,0,0]^{T},

\displaystyle=[0,-B_{x},-B_{y},-B_{z},B_{0},0,0,0]^{T}.

(347)

Using the eight-spinor gauge potentials above, we define the eight-spinor gauge-covariant derivative operators as

\displaystyle\vec{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle=\vec{\partial}-i\frac{g_{\mathrm{s}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}G_{l}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}}{2}-i\frac{g_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}W_{i}\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}}{2}-i\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}B\frac{Y_{\mathrm{w}}}{2}

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{0},\vec{\mathbf{D}}_{x},\vec{\mathbf{D}}_{y},\vec{\mathbf{D}}_{z},-\vec{\mathbf{D}}_{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}]^{T},

\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}

\displaystyle=\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\partial$}}$}+i\frac{g_{\mathrm{s}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}G_{l}\frac{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{l}}{2}+i\frac{g_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}W_{i}\frac{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{i}}{2}+i\frac{g^{\prime}_{\mathrm{ew}}}{\sqrt{\hbar c}}B\frac{Y_{\mathrm{w}}}{2}

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{0},\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}_{x},\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}_{y},\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}_{z},\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}_{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}].

(348)

The transpose in the last expression of
\vec{\mathbf{D}}
only makes this operator an eight-component column vector and it does not apply to the component matrix operators
\vec{\mathbf{D}}_{a}
.

12.3
Scalar boson

12.3.1
Standard representation

The scalar boson of the Standard Model is known as the Higgs boson with spin
S=0
. It is represented by an SU(2) doublet of complex-valued scalar fields as
Weinberg1996

\varphi=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi^{+}\\
\varphi^{0}\end{array}\right].

(349)

Here the superscripts + and 0 indicate the electric charge of the components. Through the Higgs mechanism
Higgs1964
;
Englert1964
;
Higgs1966
, the nonzero vacuum expectation value of the Higgs field results in the electroweak symmetry breaking and the generation of masses for the intermediate vector bosons and the fermions of the Standard Model
Schwartz2014
. Enabling the generation of masses while preserving the gauge symmetry of the electroweak theory
Glashow1961
;
Weinberg1967
;
Salam1968
, the Higgs mechanism became an essential part of the Standard Model.

12.3.2
Eight-spinor representation

The eight-spinor representation of the Higgs field is obtained from the standard representation above in analogy to the eight-spinor representation of the fermionic fields in equation (
336
), as

\varphi_{8}=\varphi\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}_{0}=[\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\varphi,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}]^{T}.

(350)

Again, the transpose in equation (
350
) only operates on the eight-spinor degree of freedom. The adjoint spinor is given by
\bar{\varphi}_{8}=\varphi_{8}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{0}=\varphi^{\dagger}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{e}}}_{0}=[\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},-\varphi^{\dagger},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0}]
.

12.4
Tensor boson

The Standard Model does not contain the tensor boson of spin
S=2
, the graviton, since it does not describe gravity. In the present gauge theory of unified gravity, the tensor boson associated with the four U(1) symmetry transformations of the components of the space-time dimension field in equation (
42
) is described by the tensor gauge field
H_{a\nu}
and the related field strength tensor
H_{a\mu\nu}
, introduced in equations (
58
) and (
60
).

12.5
Lagrangian density of the Standard Model extended to include gravity

We can now generalize the Lagrangian density of unified gravity, which involved the Dirac electron–positron field, electromagnetic field, and the gravitational field, to include all quantum fields of the Standard Model. Here we explicitly write the extension of the locally gauge-invariant Lagrangian density in equation (
66
). The pertinent gauge-fixed Lagrangian density would follow from the gauge-invariant Lagrangian density by the Faddeev–Popov gauge fixing method in analogy to the discussion in section
7.2
. An essential difference is that the interactions of ghost fields take a more essential role in the Yang-Mills gauge theories of the Standard Model as is well known
Peskin2018
;
Schwartz2014
.

The complete generalized gauge-invariant Lagrangian density of the Standard Model including gravity can be considered as the master Lagrangian of the Universe since it contains all known fundamental interactions of nature. It is written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{S=0}+\mathcal{L}_{S=\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{L}_{S=1}+\mathcal{L}_{S=2}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pot}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Yukawa}}.

(351)

Here
\mathcal{L}_{S=0}
,
\mathcal{L}_{S=\frac{1}{2}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{S=1}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{S=2}
are the generalized derivative terms of the spin
S=0
Higgs boson field, spin
S=\frac{1}{2}
fermion fields, spin
S=1
vector boson fields, and the spin
S=2
tensor boson gravitational field, respectively. The generalized derivative terms are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{S=0}

\displaystyle=\hbar c(i\bar{\varphi}_{8}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}_{\nu}\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}\vec{\mathbf{D}}\varphi_{8}-\bar{\varphi}_{8}\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}\vec{\mathbf{D}}\varphi_{8})\sqrt{-g},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{S=\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\frac{\hbar c}{4}\bar{\psi}_{8j}^{i}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\mathrm{F}}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}_{\nu}\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}\vec{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathbf{D}$}}$}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}_{\nu}\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{F}})\psi_{8j}^{i}\sqrt{-g},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{S=1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(i\bar{\Psi}_{i}\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathstrut\mkern 1.5mu\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}\mkern-13.0mu\raise 9.47217pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}_{\nu}^{\mkern 3.0mu\raise-3.91806pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dagger}$}}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\Psi_{i}+\bar{\Psi}_{i}\Psi_{i})\sqrt{-g},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{S=2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\kappa}H_{\rho\mu\nu}S^{\rho\mu\nu}\sqrt{-g}.

(352)

The term
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pot}}
of equation (
351
) is the Higgs field potential term, and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Yukawa}}
is the Yukawa coupling term. These terms are responsible for the generation of masses of the intermediate vector bosons and fermions. They are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{pot}}

\displaystyle=\hbar c(i\bar{\varphi}_{8}\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathstrut\mkern 1.5mu\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}\mkern-13.0mu\raise 9.47217pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}_{\nu}^{\mkern 3.0mu\raise-3.91806pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dagger}$}}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\varphi_{8}\!+\!3\bar{\varphi}_{8}\varphi_{8})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\times(\lambda_{\mathrm{H}}\bar{\varphi}_{8}\varphi_{8}\!+\!\mu_{\mathrm{H}}^{2})\sqrt{-g},

(353)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Yukawa}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\Big[-\frac{i}{2}(Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{u}})_{ij}(\bar{Q}_{\mathrm{L8}})_{i}\tilde{\varphi}\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathstrut\mkern 1.5mu\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}\mkern-13.0mu\raise 9.47217pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}_{\nu}^{\mkern 3.0mu\raise-3.91806pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dagger}$}}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}(u_{\mathrm{R8}})_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{i}{2}(Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{d}})_{ij}(\bar{Q}_{\mathrm{L8}})_{i}\varphi\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathstrut\mkern 1.5mu\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}\mkern-13.0mu\raise 9.47217pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}_{\nu}^{\mkern 3.0mu\raise-3.91806pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dagger}$}}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}(d_{\mathrm{R8}})_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt-\frac{i}{2}(Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}})_{ij}(\bar{L}_{\mathrm{L8}})_{i}\varphi\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{5}\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\mathrm{B}}^{\nu}\mathstrut\mkern 1.5mu\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}\mkern-13.0mu\raise 9.47217pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}_{\nu}^{\mkern 3.0mu\raise-3.91806pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\dagger}$}}\bar{\mathbf{I}}_{\mathrm{g}}^{\dagger}(e_{\mathrm{R8}})_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+(2Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{u}}-Y_{\mathrm{u}})_{ij}(\bar{Q}_{\mathrm{L8}})_{i}\tilde{\varphi}(u_{\mathrm{R8}})_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+(2Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{d}}-Y_{\mathrm{d}})_{ij}(\bar{Q}_{\mathrm{L8}})_{i}\varphi(d_{\mathrm{R8}})_{j}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+(2Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}-Y_{\mathrm{e}})_{ij}(\bar{L}_{\mathrm{L8}})_{i}\varphi(e_{\mathrm{R8}})_{j}+h.c.\Big]\sqrt{-g}.

(354)

Here
\tilde{\varphi}=i\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\varphi^{*}
is the charge conjugate state of the Higgs doublet,
\mu_{\mathrm{H}}
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{H}}
are parameters of the Higgs potential,
Y_{\mathrm{u}}
,
Y_{\mathrm{d}}
, and
Y_{\mathrm{e}}
are
3\times 3
Yukawa coupling matrices corresponding to the inertial masses,
Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{u}}
,
Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{d}}
, and
Y^{\prime}_{\mathrm{e}}
are Yukawa coupling matrices corresponding to the gravitational masses, and
h.c.
denotes the Hermitian conjugate of the preceding terms. The gravitational gauge field
H_{a\nu}
enters the theory trough the gravitational-gauge covariant derivative
\vec{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{D}}}_{\nu}
, defined in equation (
58
). This derivative preserves its form when all interactions of the Standard Model are included in the theory. Thus the Lagrangian densities in equations (
66
) and (
351
) are invariant in the same local U(1) symmetry transformation of equation (
42
).

The relation between the Lagrangian density of the gauge theory of gravity for QED in equation (
66
) and the complete Lagrangian density of the Standard Model including gravity in equation (
351
) is the following: The first term of equation (
66
), the derivative term of the Dirac

electron–positron field, is described through
\mathcal{L}_{S=\frac{1}{2}}
, where the right- and left-handed electron–positron fields
e_{\mathrm{R}}
and
e_{\mathrm{L}}
in
\psi_{j}^{i}
, defined in equation (
335
), give the contribution of the Dirac electron–positron field spinor
\psi
of equation (
66
). The second and third terms of equation (
66
), the mass terms of the Dirac field, are described through the Yukawa coupling
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Yukawa}}
. The fourth and fifth terms of equation (
66
), the derivative terms of the electromagnetic field, are described through
\mathcal{L}_{S=1}
, where the linear combination of
\mathcal{W}_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}
terms of
\Psi_{i}
, defined in equation (
344
), forms the electromagnetic field spinor
\Psi
of equation (
66
). The interaction between the Dirac and electromagnetic fields is described through the gauge-covariant derivative. The sixth term of equation (
66
), the derivative term of the gravitational gauge field, is equal to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{g,kin}}=\mathcal{L}_{S=2}
, given in equation (
64
).
